#7363. Salesloft: Salesforce Business Systems Administrator

This is a fully remote opportunity and can be worked from any location in the United States.

Salesloft is building the next era of enterprise revenue — one where teams make confident decisions powered by AI and real signals. By combining our scale, insights, and AI innovation, we're building the industry's first Predictive Revenue System, enabling humans and AI to work together to make smarter decisions and drive consistent growth.

With thousands of customers using our platforms every day, we have an unmatched view into how revenue is actually won — the Revenue Context that reveals what happens, when, and with what outcome. This gives us a unique opportunity to transform an entire category and set a new benchmark for how modern revenue teams operate.

Join us to help transform how companies around the world run revenue — and build the platform that will guide leading revenue teams into the future.

At Salesloft, our Business Systems Administrator, Customer Experience is critical to our company’s success. You will be a key member of our fast-growing and high-performing Business Systems team and will lead generating, scoping, solutioning, and executing process and efficiency improvement efforts across customer-focused systems. You will have a passionate, capable team supporting you.

You will be responsible for the support, development, and maintenance of Salesforce and its related Customer Experience systems. You must own the pursuit of a world-class technology experience for our customers, while enabling scale and efficiency for internal customer experience teams - Support, Services, and Success. You need to have pride in the work you do and a passion for customer support and success.

This role lives at the heart of how we Make Your Mark: you'll treat systems administration as a discipline — automating, documenting, and fixing root causes so the same issues don't recur. And in the spirit of how we are Customer Inspired — where every decision has a true north, and it's our customer — you'll administer access and configuration as the security controls they are, protecting the company and the data customers trust us with.

We are seeking a Salesforce CRM, and related customer experience systems, expert. Someone that can see the big picture, future state but has a keen eye for details. You will have a knack for spotting opportunities to improve systems and process, and are excited to work cross-functionally to make it happen. You are passionate to build relationships to help others succeed.

If you’re looking for an opportunity to learn more, do more, and become more, then becoming aBusiness Systems Administrator is the career path for you!

Our Salesloft’s Revenue Operations team is comprised of seasoned and up-and-coming operational experts.

Values: Move as One, Evidence over Ego, Lead from the Front, Make Your Mark, Customer-Inspired.

The Revenue Operations team consists of team members that work with Sales, Marketing, Customer Services, Finance, and IT to create systems to more efficiently prospect, forecast, close, onboard, and support customers. Team members conceptualize the strategic priorities for the company, develop execution plans, execute plans cross-functionally, and operationalize executed strategies for continuous improvement.

At Salesloft, we are committed to creating an inclusive and supportive workplace where everyone belongs and can thrive. We focus on culture add, not culture fit, and believe our teams are made stronger by the unique perspectives, experiences, and identities each person brings.

We are proud to be an Equal Opportunity Employer and provide employment opportunities to all employees and applicants without regard to race, color, religion, sex, sexual orientation, gender identity, national origin, age, disability, veteran status, pregnancy, or any other characteristic protected by law.

If you're excited about this role even though your experience may not perfectly match every requirement, we encourage you to apply. We are actively hiring across multiple geographies and would love to welcome passionate, curious, and mission-driven individuals to our growing team. Explore our open roles and consider joining us!

Please note that all official communication regarding job opportunities at Salesloft will come from an @salesloft.com email address. If you receive messages on LinkedIn or other job platforms claiming to be from Salesloft, they may not be legitimate. To verify the authenticity of any job-related communication, please visit our official Careers Page (https://salesloft.com/company/careers/).

#7362. YouTube expands custom feeds and offers new partnership options

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The platform is trying to make it easier for users to access personally relevant discovery options using artificial intelligence-enabled responses.

YouTube updated its in-app discovery listings and also integrated its artificial intelligence-powered Ask Studio guidance with its support tools to make it easier to manage queries in a single workflow. The company is also testing product recommendations for potential creator partnerships.

First, YouTube is expanding its custom feed listings in order to make it easier for users to create and access personally relevant feeds based on conversational prompts.

Currently available to U.S. users, YouTube’s custom feed generation option provides another way to define the in-app experience by enabling users to tell YouTube’s AI system what they want to see.

YouTube is now making these custom feeds easier to access by adding them to the top navigation bar.

These custom feeds will also replace non-custom topic options in the top listing. YouTube's also moving the subscription and standard feed options, bringing them together into the top-of-feed display.

That should make it easier for users to integrate this AI-powered option and help them build a more personally relevant in-app experience.

For creators, YouTube is rolling out an integrated Ask Studio and YouTube Help chat element. This should make it easier for creators to generate AI guidance for their channel and connect with YouTube’s internal support for other queries.

YouTube’s Head of Editorial Rene Ritchie said this will mean that creators no longer have to switch between chat windows for each element.

YouTube is also testing product recommendations for brand content partnerships. These are personalized to each channel and could help creators find more relevant sponsorship opportunities.

As explained by Ritchie: “If you're an eligible affiliate creator, YouTube Studio is rolling out a brand new personalized product recommendation shelf right inside your shopping hub. These recommendations are based on your channel's past content and audience engagement and what's trending in your niche, like beauty, tech, or home.”

The new display will also show estimated commission rates for each potential partnership, providing more assistance for creators when considering their opportunities.

Finally, YouTube is expanding its Hype with Jewels creator donation option to more markets.

This is a combination of two products: YouTube’s Jewels are essentially an in-app currency that viewers can use to buy in-app digital stickers, which they can then send to creators to show their support during a livestream.

Users can purchase packages of Jewels, and creators get a cut from any sticker allocated during their stream.

YouTube’s Hype program, meanwhile, enables viewers to allocate Hype points to a video in order to boost its ranking and help it get seen by more people.

Hype with Jewels lets creators use purchased Jewels to add more Hype to videos, which will then help them get seen by more people.

As per YouTube: “Starting November 6, 2026, we’re expanding Hype with Jewels to eligible creators and viewers in the United States, Canada, Germany, Ireland, Italy, Poland, Spain, Turkey, and the United Kingdom.”

YouTube is also expanding the Hype limits for streams, with viewers now able to allocate as much Hype as they like to streamers.

#7361. Director, Data Science at DuckDuckGo

Hi, we're DuckDuckGo, the online protection company and remote-first team of 400+ on a mission to raise the standard of trust online. Founded in 2008 and profitable since 2014, our annual revenue is in the hundreds of millions USD and millions use our browser on Mac, Windows, iOS, and Android, our search engine, and the DuckDuckGo subscription. We also offer private, useful, and optional AI, including Duck.ai, which lets you chat privately with ChatGPT, Claude, and other AIs, all in one place.

Our culture of trust, inclusivity, and empowered project management underpins everything we do, where each team member takes full ownership of their projects, from scoping and execution to postmortem. If you're seeking end-to-end ownership of your work, you've come to the right place!

Working on the Data Science Functional Team, you'll drive product and business impact by developing data scientists, setting clear expectations for them, providing hands-on mentorship, and removing blockers.

You have 10+ years of experience in data science or ML engineering, with 4+ years leading teams, and a track record of setting multi-team roadmaps and adapting swiftly as priorities shift.

You've driven organizational culture toward data-driven decision-making and can translate technical tradeoffs and risks clearly for executives and cross-functional stakeholders.

You enjoy coaching experienced data scientists who are already highly skilled. You give direct, actionable feedback and you've built teams where people do the best work of their careers.

You write production-grade code in Python or a comparable high-level language and stay close to the technical work when it matters most.

You have advanced SQL and query optimization skills, familiarity with columnar databases like BigQuery, Clickhouse, Redshift, or Druid.

Preferred: experience at a consumer-facing tech company where data or analytics is core to the product strategy.

This role is paid on a regional pay scale, in local currency, and stock options:

Europe/Rest of World: €214,950 EUR annually

In Spain, pay is expected to transition to the EUR scale on January 1, 2027, and in other Europe/Rest of World countries on February 1, 2027.

Compensation is transparent across the organization, and all team members within the same professional level and global region receive the same compensation.

Our Team Member Support Guide explains how we prioritize your wellbeing including paid parental leave, office setup, and co-working allowances.

Hiring works best when it's a two-way street. Learn how we help you get to know DuckDuckGo, envision your future role here, and find out more about how we hire.

DuckDuckGo provides equal work opportunities to all team members and applicants, and it prohibits discrimination and harassment of any type on the basis of race, color, ethnicity, caste, religion, age, sex (including pregnancy), national origin, disability status, genetics, protected veteran status, sexual orientation, gender identity or expression, or any other characteristic protected by our policies or federal, state, or local laws.

We want to ensure that our hiring process is accessible. If you need reasonable accommodation for any part of the application process because of a medical condition or disability, please send an email to careers@duckduckgo.com to let us know the nature of your request.

You’ll be required to attend meetings on camera via video conferencing

Expect to travel at least two times a year: once for our all-hands meetup and again for a team retreat (each around 4-5 days). While extenuating circumstances may impact attendance, everyone is strongly encouraged to attend.

While we offer a flexible work arrangement with no core hours, expect an average full-time commitment of 40 hours per week.

A successful candidate must pass a background check as a condition of joining the team.

By applying for this role, you confirm that all information submitted is accurate and complete. You further acknowledge that providing false or fraudulent information during the application process is cause for denial of an offer, revocation of any existing offer, or other adverse action, up to and including termination after the start of your commencement of work.

We use AI to flag potentially fraudulent applications, analyze and summarize applicants’ experience, interviews, and project performance, and help streamline our selection process.

Human Oversight and Accountability: The AI technology is designed to support our hiring team by providing insights and summaries of applications and evaluations of test projects against scoring rubrics. All final evaluations and hiring decisions, however, will be made by our hiring team, who will consider the AI's input alongside other factors.

Transparency: We believe in transparency regarding our hiring practices. If you have any questions about how AI is used in our recruitment process, please feel free to reach out to us.

#7359. Lenovo NA CMO on telling the ‘full stack story’ through sports

The World Cup drove 4.4 billion social impressions and a 4.2% lift in brand value for the tech company.

Lenovo used its FIFA sponsorship to put its artificial intelligence tools in front of billions of viewers. From the Lenovo AI Referee View to an ad campaign featuring soccer superstar David Beckham, the tech company looked to put itself front-and-center during one of the most watched sports programs in the world. And it worked, earning the brand 4.4 billion social impressions and 1.8 billion creator/influencer impressions, per data provided by the brand.

Over the course of the tournament, Lenovo said it saw a 4.2 times lift in brand consideration among tech-focused soccer fans and a 4.2% lift in brand value. It also claims to have captured the top competitive share of voice among FIFA partners on social media and driven 460,000 page views to Lenovo-owned sites.

Lenovo North America CMO Milo Speranzo sat down with Marketing Dive to talk about making the most of expensive sports sponsorships, how the brand is looking to replicate success with the women’s soccer tournament in 2027 and how it's differentiating itself in the AI race.

The following interview has been edited for brevity and clarity.

MILO SPERANZO: Having the same goals, the same messaging and storytelling, as well as the ability for us to integrate our product and technology into the actual event was a key pillar in that decision making of, do we go all in? With FIFA and the World Cup, there's no partial in, or at least doesn't feel like it.

It's an all-in engagement, and when we made that decision in 2024, we were very confident that we had covered all three of our determination silos that we look at in order to really ask ourselves: Will this be a successful partnership? [Those silos are] tech integration, can we tell an authentic story and then the marketing KPIs on the back half. Those three pillars for the World Cup just lined up perfectly.

Yes, it was. When we look at a return on investment for a partnership, the brand piece is a third of what we judge ourselves on. I would say that all of our marketing-specific KPIs were exceeded. It was very much an awareness play, as well as educating our customers that we are not only products. We are a very deeply resourced supply chain-resilient technology solutions company that has a massive AI portfolio.

David is an amazing representative of our brand. He is a wonderful personality for one, but two, he also has an eclectic mix of hobbies. He’s a chicken farmer, small business owner, he builds motorcycles. He embodies what we would consider a potential super-user, and he does use Lenovo. He's very much a brand ambassador of our product line, so when we looked at potential spokespersons for the World Cup, the Beckham-soccer fit was very natural. Lenovo is a global brand. FIFA is a global property, so we did not look at it as a U.S. spokesperson. We looked at it as a global spokesperson.

It's a very different audience. [For the men] we saw massive North America engagement when the North America countries were engaged. We saw engagement stay higher than expectations, drop significantly after the U.S. and Canada were out.

For the women, our U.S. and Canadian teams are just powerhouses. During the men’s tournament, we planned for a lot of our early bespoke, larger events, larger engagements to be really in those early to quarterfinal rounds, and then transitioned to a lot of branding and hospitality as we got into the semis and the finals. For the Women's World Cup, we have to think of it very differently because the eyes of my audience here in North America, I believe, are going to be affixed to this equally across the entire tournament, hopefully all the way into the finals.

For us in North America, the marketing KPIs for the Women's World Cup we feel will exceed what we saw during the men's. That’s for the reasons we just spoke about. Canada and the U.S. women are powerhouses. So, we're really excited to double down in areas that maybe we didn't double down during the men's World Cup.

The men's had more countries, it had more games. There's less [with the Women’s World Cup]. We have a chance to focus our energy, especially our marketing energy, on fewer, bigger campaigns. It'll be everything from bespoke events to hospitality, just being there, to physical sponsorships, to fan fests. There's going to be a couple large fan fests in Rio de Janeiro and Sao Paulo that we’ll have a pretty big presence at.

As far as differentiation in the tech stack, everybody says they're everything to everybody. In reality, from an ecosystem perspective, Lenovo is the only technology provider that can provide everything from phone to PC to all the adjacencies, the cold screens and mice and portables and battery backups, all the way to the most complex data center solutions.

The answer [to standing out] is storytelling. You'll see a lot of tech companies attach themselves to a brand or a sports brand or a property, but only tell that story as it resonates with the sport. We need to tell that story so it resonates beyond the sports where you're just not getting the value that you should get out of what is a very expensive partnership.

#7357. TikTok shares gaming and app promotional guides for end-of-year period

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The company says the period between Christmas and New Year offers brands an opportunity to help consumers buy something for themselves.

TikTok has published two new guides covering the end-of-the-year period between Christmas and New Year, which is often referred to as Q5. The playbooks offer tips on how to maximize gaming and app promotions.

TikTok said consumers are highly active and seeking entertainment during this period, which could be a big opportunity for gaming and app companies.

As per TikTok: “Seventy percent of consumers plan to shop during the week immediately after December 25 to take advantage of sales and redeem gift cards. On TikTok, 75% of users spend the same amount of time or more shopping during Q5, while 63% shop specifically for themselves.”

For gaming brands, TikTok’s four-page guide highlights a lower cost per ad during the Q5 period due to lower competition.

The guide also includes tips on how to set up specific campaign types, as well as overall notes on how gaming brands can maximize their opportunities.

TikTok’s Q5 app marketing guide includes similar notes, as well as info on tracking setup and best practices for different promotion types.

Combined, the two handbooks provide technical, helpful guidance for companies seeking to maximize their end-of-year promotions, with practical tips on campaign implementation and tracking.

#7355. Wendy’s takes a shot at McDonald’s with rewards-based video game

“WendyQuest: Legend of the Four Corner Stones” challenges players to defeat enemies like the Golden Archers, and arrives as the chain chases a turnaround.

Wendy’s is continuing to target gaming communities with the launch of “WendyQuest,” its first fully-owned game. The standalone experience could help Wendy’s capture stronger engagement as the in-game advertising landscape grows saturated: Spending on in-game ads is expected to top $131 billion in 2026 and grow to over $217 billion by 2031.

“WendyQuest: Legend of the Four Corner Stones” involves puzzles with turn-based battles, challenging players to go up against battle mechs, or oversized combat vehicles, including the Mech Chicken — a clear shot at rival McDonald’s. Players can power up with Jr. Bacon Cheeseburgers while navigating five food-focused regions, each of which offers a chance to earn Wendy’s food offers.

The effort offers up to 10 hours of gameplay and was built by hand over several years without the help of artificial intelligence shortcuts, according to the brand. Available prizes include a free medium fry, small frosty, 10 piece nuggets, biscuit or croissant or Dave’s Single. To redeem the reward, consumers need to copy a promo code from the inventory section of the game and enter it into the offers section of the Wendy’s app.

The launch of “WendyQuest” follows other gaming-focused efforts from the chain, including a tabletop role playing game in 2019 called “Feast of Legends,” which challenged players to fight against frozen beef. The chain also activated in Fortnite’s Food Fight game mode.

Other marketers have sought to appeal to gamers, from Panda Express to E.l.f. Cosmetics. Chili’s launched an online video game in 2024 digging at fast food restaurants that have jacked up prices. Gaming represents a valuable opportunity for brands looking to reach a wider audience: two-thirds of Americans between the ages of 5 and 90 game at least one hour per week, according to 2026 data from the Entertainment Software Association.

Wendy’s is currently in the midst of deploying a turnaround plan following ongoing challenges related to traffic and sales growth. The turnaround, which includes a focus on creating distinctive branding and marketing, is being led by Bob Wright, who has served as president and CEO since May. The chain in August brought on ex-McDonald’s CMO Tariq Hassan as chief marketing and customer growth officer. McDonald’s recently unveiled a new strategic plan that sees it shifting its approach to advertising and loyalty.

Wendy’s on Nov. 5 reported its third-quarter earnings, which include a 5.7% decrease in U.S. same-restaurant sales and a 2.2% increase in international same-restaurant sales, according to an earnings release.

#7354. Senior Software Engineer, API Products at 0x

At 0x, our mission is to "Create a Tokenized World Where All Forms of Value Can Flow Freely". We deliver this to web3 builders with a delightfully simple suite of developer APIs that provide faster trading, better prices, and superior user experience.

Founded in 2017, we’ve processed over 211M+ transactions and $180B+ in trading volume from users trading on Matcha, Coinbase Wallet, Robinhood Wallet, Phantom, Rainbow, DefiLlama, MetaMask, Zerion, Zapper, and many more. Our investors include Greylock Partners, Pantera Capital, and Jump Crypto.

When you join 0x, you’ll become part of a veteran team of crypto builders who know the importance of code quality, team cohesion, and a culture of learning.

Behind every 0x trade is a lot of hard work. We integrate hundreds of liquidity sources, optimize for gas, slippage and latency, split routes and simulate every result to get traders the best possible outcome. The API Products team turns that routing logic into easy-to-integrate APIs that wallets, exchanges, trading apps, bridges and developers around the world rely on.

This team of four built, maintains and continues to evolve our flagship Swap, Gasless and Cross-Chain APIs, which have moved billions in volume. Next, they’re building new products that give developers even more tools for building trading apps.

We’re hiring a Senior Software Engineer to expand what the API Products team can take on. You’ll lead projects from first idea to production, on new products and on the APIs that are already live, working closely with our product team and some of our biggest customers. This is a hands-on IC role for someone who wants real ownership and works well with little direction. Remote, EU timezones preferred.

Build new APIs and trading products, from researching the problem and designing the architecture to shipping the first version.

Maintain and extend our existing APIs, including support for new chains, bridges and customer-requested features.

Keep our APIs fast and reliable as traffic grows, and participate in the on-call rotation.

Investigate failed trades and stuck transfers using logs, traces and onchain data, and resolve the root cause.

Work with product, legal and GTM, turning business and compliance needs into technical decisions.

Write design documents for your work and review your teammates’ designs and pull requests.

6+ years of backend engineering experience, including at least 2 years in DeFi at a trading app, wallet, DEX aggregator, onchain data company or similar.

Senior or Lead Engineer with a track record of owning complex projects end to end.

Built and maintained developer-facing APIs.

Deep onchain knowledge, especially EVM: how transactions work, including calldata, contract interfaces, gas and approvals.

Strong system design experience for production-scale backend services.

You’re an AI power user who uses it to multiply your impact as an engineer, yet you don’t let it replace your judgment.

Clear communicator, comfortable explaining technical tradeoffs to non-engineers.

Aligned with our core values: do the right thing, consistently ship, and focus on long-term impact.

Able to attend annual in-person team offsites around the globe.

Experience with cross-chain/bridging, or DEX aggregation.

Experience with other ecosystems outside of EVM such as Solana.

Experience with TypeScript and/or Rust, our main languages.

The base salary range for this position is USD $150,000 - $230,000 + equity + ZRX tokens + benefits. Within the range, individual pay is determined by job-related skills, experience, location, business needs, and candidate preferences between the different compensation elements.

Comprehensive insurance (medical/dental/vision/life/disability) for U.S.-based employees — 100% of base plan covered for you and dependents

Monthly mobile phone bill, wellness, and pre-tax transportation expense

Covered mental health benefits (included professional therapy sessions)

Lunch reimbursement for all employees across the globe!

Stipend for your ideal remote / WFH set-up: headphones, and any other work gear you may need

Great office conveniently located in the SF Financial District for those in the region!

Flexible vacation: Take time when you need it (and we really mean it!)

0x and its associated entities are dedicated to fostering diversity, inclusion, and belonging in its teams and workforce, and are proud to be equal opportunity employers. 0x does not make employment or hiring decisions on the basis of race, color, creed, religion, sex (including those who are pregnant or have given birth), sexual orientation, gender, gender expression or identity, age, disability, medical condition, genetic information, military or veteran status, marital status, pregnancy, citizenship, national origin, immigration or citizenship status, political affiliation, or any other basis that is protected by applicable local, state, or federal laws. #7352. Meta adds mini app creation option to Muse

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

New functionality allows Meta’s artificial intelligence agent to store task trackers and convert commands into custom interfaces.

Meta added a new option to its Muse artificial intelligence agents that will enable users to create mini apps addressing whatever topic they choose.

As outlined by Alex Cornell, a product designer for Muse, Muse Spaces are dedicated commands converted into their own interface, which makes them easy to revisit for ongoing tracking.

To create a Space, Muse users tell the agent to create a Space based on whatever parameters they want.

Muse will then store these trackers as mini apps within the Muse sidebar.

That could make it easier to stay updated on whatever information the user chooses, with Meta providing a range of potential use cases:

Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg said that he had created a Muse Space that controls the vents on his meat smoker via Bluetooth.

As per Meta: “Got a half-finished idea sitting in your notes app, a spreadsheet you keep meaning to clean up, or a hobby you want to dive into? Chances are you can turn it into a space.”

The option could lead to some interesting use cases, and could help users streamline and improve their task management.

Meta also added expanded memory to Muse and Meta AI, which should better enable these AI tools to provide personalized responses based on advanced systematic understanding of each user.

Meta’s Active Memory and Active Focus features, which were originally announced as part of its Muse Wonder update, will extend the capacity of its AI tools to recall previously mentioned details in chats.

As per Meta: “Active Memory enables Muse and Meta AI to remember what matters and recognize when earlier information becomes relevant, making it easier to build off your past conversations. We’ve trained our models to recognize what type of information is temporary versus permanent, to reason across time, and to distinguish between what is still relevant and what has become stale.”

Active Focus, meanwhile, enables Meta’s Muse agents to continue working toward a goal, even if the agents are faced with unexpected challenges.

“We’ve trained our models on a variety of bottlenecks reflective of the real world, so that they can try harder, recover, or find alternatives on your behalf. In Muse, our models show a 67% improvement in completing everyday tasks where unexpected issues arise.”

That could help to ensure Muse gets more things done, though there have also been reports of empowered other AI agents breaking through system security measures to complete an assigned task.

As reported by Wired, OpenAI had to revise its containment measures recently after a set of rogue AI breached another company’s system.

Meta recently reported that the Muse app has already exceeded 10 million downloads, and that shopping was its primary use case.

Analysis from app data provider Sensor Tower, however, has shown that user retention for Muse dips significantly two weeks after download, which means that Meta will need to keep highlighting practical use cases to maintain interest in the app.

#7348. Sociable: Reddit tests ads in search results

The platform wants to help advertisers align their promotions with users’ discovery behavior even as the company’s overall search traffic drops.

“Sociable” is the latest commentary on important social media developments and trends from industry expert Andrew Hutchinson of Social Media Today.

Reddit is testing ads in search results in the app, as the platform looks for more ways to help advertisers align with discovery behaviors.

As shown in these example screens, Reddit’s search ads will be targeted by query, and will include a small ad marker in the upper section of the panel.

As explained by Reddit: “People come to Reddit seeking human perspectives and experiences they can’t find elsewhere, with half of Reddit users saying the platform helps them discover something new. As we continue improving the user search experience on Reddit, we’re also beginning to test ad placements in search. These placements leverage existing ad targeting in search results pages on Reddit, which have high purchase and search intent.”

Reddit said the promotions will be shown in search results based on a range of factors, though these will primarily be aligned with contextual relevance.

Reddit has been working to improve its discovery tools as the app tries to help users find more relevant matches for their queries.

In July, Reddit published a study that found that 72% of its users come to the platform seeking information, and 64% of them have a specific goal in mind.

That also aligns with how Reddit gains traffic from search results, since the platform gets significant citation volume from AI chatbots.

However, AI traffic has started to dry up. Last week, as part of its Q3 performance update, Reddit CEO Steve Huffman said that the company’s search traffic dropped by more than 1 million referrals per week on average in October.

That could reduce the value of its own in-app discovery process, though Tuesday’s announcement suggests that Reddit is clearly hoping to maintain its value as a product discovery tool.

Reddit said that it will initially test ads in search with a select group of eligible advertisers.

#7345. Agencies find AI overuse is leading to brief bloat

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is part of Digiday’s coverage of its Digiday Media Buying Summit. More from the series →

The advertising world rushed to embrace generative AI. But now that agencies and advertisers have come to rely on the tech to keep up, they’re finding themselves vulnerable to its toxic byproducts.

In particular, agency leaders are finding the over-use of AI tools to write advertising briefs risks undermining the productivity and the day-to-day sanity of marketing staffers.

According to M&C Saatchi’s North American CEO, Nadja Bellan-White, over-reliance on AI for brief writing is approaching “a tipping point… It’s a little bit ridiculous.” Speaking during last week’s Digiday Media Buying Summit, she estimated that 85% of the briefs sent to the agency by clients were written using AI tools.

The impact? Corporate documents that run into the hundreds of pages, replete with marketing jargon but little actual vision. “How many many times can you say ‘framework’ in one sentence? How many times can you try ‘economies of scale’? How many times can you tell me I need this to ‘tie back to commercial objectives’?”

Brief writing has become one of the most common applications for AI tech in marketing. More than half (56.4%) of marketers and agencies now use it to build out strategy, media or campaign creative briefs, according to a survey conducted by Basis Technologies between July and August.

Media agencies use agents to suggest audience segments, run analyses of competitors’ media plans or suggest search keywords to bid on. Creative shops and influencer agencies use AI to skip through reams of consumer research, brand guidelines and economic trends, or to find ways of trumping received marketing wisdom. In-house teams at high-spending brands use generative tools to spin up strategy approaches. Even publishers now use agentic tools that respond to client briefs with AI-generated insights.

Staffers will point to the hours clawed back or to the insights discovered from a broader or deeper reading of research. Behind closed doors, however, they kvetch about the cost.

“I’ve seen some of the worst briefs I’ve ever seen in my career recently, and it’s because they’re so disjointed and there’s no through-line on what… the marketing goal is,” said one agency executive, speaking under Chatham House rules at a townhall session at DMBS.

“AI is making it worse. It’s pontificating on a sentence structure that makes [a brief] 10 pages long,” said another.

Complaints about the proliferation of memos or emails, or indeed badly written campaign briefs, aren’t new. At the start of the decade, industry veterans will recall there was even a cross-industry initiative to encourage better brief writing. But just as it’s sped up the production of digital creative assets, Claude and ChatGPT tools have enabled bad writing to spread like knotweed. As another agency exec put it: “There’s a lack of critical thinking.”

Industry leaders are beginning to acknowledge those problems in public. “[AI] can be a good sparring partner,” said Bellan-White. “But at the end of the day, you need to be the one to make the decision. You understand the client’s briefs. You understand the market context. You’ve got to really think about what it is that’s recommended.”

It’s not hard to see how AI has become a crutch. Good briefs take time to craft, time that clients are loath to grant their agencies, or to use themselves, for something so far removed from the end product. Little surprise, then, that staffers turn to a solution that does the work for them like a white-collar version of the shoemaker’s elves.

In the Brothers Grimm tale, however, the shoemaker eventually retakes control of production. But advertising staffers are long-term practitioners of what academics call “cognitive offloading,” the delegation of knowledge work to a tool or machine.

A growing body of research suggests that such over-reliance on AI tools literally reduces the cognitive abilities of users. One 2025 MIT Media Lab study, for example, measured the brainwaves of students writing essays without the aid of technology, compared with those writing with the assistance of search engines or ChatGPT. The AI-assisted group showed a 55% reduction in brainwave activity.

Whatever the name, the signs are the same. A profusion of em-dashes, paragraph upon paragraph of empty, over-long sentences, the phrase “it’s not X, it’s Y.”

Cognitive offloading isn’t just an abstract (or aesthetic) problem for ad agency leaders, some of whom are recognizing that some marketing staffers have gone from using AI as an aid to relying upon it to prop them up. Bloated documents steal time from colleagues or clients, while many users’ loose attitude to the statistics AI tools conjure up demand for a closer-than usual supervision by fact-checkers. According to a GWI survey of 150 British marketers published last month, 29% admitted inaccurate or misleading insights had cost their companies financially.

“They have exported all of their thinking to AI,” said Tony Russo, vp, innovation and strategy at indie agency Mile Marker, during another DMBS session. “It’s a judgment problem.”

If there is a solution, it’s an offline one. Christina Riccitelli, chief innovation and analytics officer at media agency OpAD, said managers should intervene if they found junior staff leaning too hard on AI. “You have to reel them back in,” she said.

Bellan-White recommended more drastic action. “Turn off your laptop. Just literally shut it down. Turn off your phones,” she said. “I want you to look me in the eye and actually have a conversation. Do you know how uncomfortable that is for people today? Because if you actually have to have a conversation, that means you actually have to know what you’re talking about.”

Big names like actor Terry Crews and athletes could soon appear in AI-generated ads across the web. But they will be marked as such.

Advertisers now demand creator metrics like cost per lead and conversions, not follower counts.

The AI platform is testing an analytics dashboard that counts how often a business shows up in ChatGPT’s answers.

Get access to tools and analysis to stay ahead of the trends transforming media and marketing

Visit your account page to make changes and renew.

Get Digiday's top stories every morning in your email inbox.

Follow @Digiday for the latest news, insider access to events and more.

#7344. SDK Engineer - Python at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We're looking for a SDK Engineer - Python to join our SDK team and own how developers talk to Supabase from Python. You'll work closely with the rest of the SDK team, our Auth, Realtime, Storage, PostgREST, Functions, CLI and Docs teams, and the open-source community, helping us make the Python SDK a library Python developers genuinely enjoy using.

Python developers reach Supabase from backend services, data pipelines, notebooks, and a growing number of AI agents and agent frameworks. The Python SDK is how all of them talk to the platform: database queries, auth, storage, realtime and edge functions, in both sync and async flavors.

This is a library-authoring role, and the work happens in the open: public repos, public issue trackers, and API decisions whose consequences last for years. If you get satisfaction from a type checker that catches the bug before your users do, from an async client that cleans up after itself, and from an issue tracker that isn't a graveyard, you'll like it here.

This role is ideal for someone who thrives in async, fast-paced environments and is excited about building developer tools that scale to millions.

Build and evolve our Python SDK across database, auth, storage, realtime and functions, with both sync and async APIs that feel natural in Python.

Start new things. Alongside the maintenance work, find what's missing, make the case for it, and build it. Nobody will hand you the list.

Work in the open. Triage inbound issues, review and shepherd outside contributions, keep CI and release automation healthy, and hold a clear, kind line on scope.

Own the typing experience. Make the SDK fully typed and checker-friendly, so autocomplete and static analysis stay correct in real codebases.

Keep us honest across environments. Multiple Python versions, sync and async frameworks, workers, serverless and notebooks — with dependency ranges that don't fight our users' projects. Test it, don't assume it.

Work on realtime. Long-lived websocket connections, reconnection, and subscription and presence semantics.

Track the platform. When auth, realtime, storage, postgrest or edge functions ship a change, make sure the SDK reflects it, and that we say so in the changelog.

Design in public. Write the RFCs, migration guides, release notes and docs. Treat a breaking change as a deprecation path plus a migration story, not a version bump.

Make our SDK legible to AI coding agents, which are now a major consumer of it.

Know modern Python typing deeply. You use generics, protocols, overloads and TypedDict as design tools, run strict type checking, and fix type errors without reaching for Any.

Have authored or maintained a published library with a public API and users outside your own company. You know what it costs to change something people depend on.

Know the maintainer side of open source. Issue triage at volume, reviewing outside PRs, release automation, changelogs and semver, security reports, and saying no to a feature request without losing the person who asked.

Are fluent in asyncio and have opinions on how a library should serve both sync and async callers.

Know Python packaging for libraries: pyproject.toml, wheels, modern tooling, version support policy, and why libraries shouldn't pin their dependencies tightly.

Test as a habit: unit, type-level and end-to-end against a real stack. Every fix comes with a regression test.

Write well. Design docs, changelogs and issue replies are a large part of the output here, much of it public, and we're async by default.

Have real autonomy: you find the important work, scope it, ship it, and say what you didn't do.

Communicate clearly across both technical and non-technical audiences

Have experience in async or globally distributed teams

Are energized by solving real-world problems for developers

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and moving quickly

Bonus points if you: have run a major-version migration for a public package, know your way around JWT/OAuth, have built integrations for AI agent frameworks, or have generated idiomatic clients from a schema.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

#7342. L’Oréal’s evolving microdrama strategy puts entertainment over product

A ReelShort series from Urban Decay themed around ghosting dates generated 3.8 million views in five days.

L’Oréal was early to the branded microdrama boom in the U.S., running a holiday effort for Maybelline last year that riffed on Hallmark romance movie tropes. The beauty and cosmetics giant around Halloween again dipped its toe into the short-form video genre that has ballooned in popularity as marketers produce entertainment they hope can attract scroll-oriented viewers but requires a different touch than a glossy ad campaign.

The company’s Urban Decay brand on Oct. 24 debuted a vertical video series on ReelShort titled “Ghost Hunter Hotline,” which runs six episodes that generally last just a few minutes each. The story follows protagonist Zoe as she dishes out justice to romantic interests who suddenly went silent on the communications front, or “ghosted” her and her friends, while showing off Urban Decay’s aptly named Ghosting Spray product. A former fling eventually returns and throws a wrench in some of Zoe’s plans, the type of soapy twist typical to cliffhanger-heavy microdramas. The overarching idea was to blend elements typical to the spooky season with tongue-in-cheek commentary on modern dating culture.

“Ghost Hunter Hotline” generated 3.8 million views in the five-day window after launch while sitting at No. 2 on ReelShort’s most-viewed shorts ranking, according to data shared by the brand. L’Oréal’s microdrama experiments are part of a larger effort to think differently from an old-school advertising mindset that puts the chief focus on product messaging and brand story.

“It’s something that we’ve now known in the creator era for the last couple of years, which is that you really need to think about your audience first, and you think about entertainment first as the primary purpose [of marketing],” said Han Wen, chief digital and marketing officer at L’Oréal USA.

Rather than create the microdrama content with an external agency, as it had done in the past, L’Oréal produced, developed and distributed “Ghost Hunter Hotline” in collaboration with ReelShort, relying on its knowledge of what appeals to the genre’s audience. ReelShort, which is owned by Crazy Maple Studio, is one of the leading dedicated microdrama platforms, on track to generate over $1 billion in revenue this year, according to third-party industry analyses.

“We went into it knowing that we wanted to test directly with a platform rather than a more hybrid format,” said Wen.

Urban Decay proved to be the right fit for the content type in a L’Oréal portfolio that spans dozens of brands. Halloween is a major occasion for the luxury label, which was founded in the mid-’90s in Los Angeles and promotes an edgier, antibland image. L’Oréal acquired Urban Decay in 2012 to reach higher-end young shoppers.

Other marketers are turning to microdramas because they can be turned around quickly and on the cheap. “Ghost Hunter Hotline” came together faster than a traditional ad campaign, in about four weeks, according to Wen.

“Speed is really important in the current context of marketing, where trends move very quickly,” said Wen.

Wen acknowledged that not all of L’Oréal’s bets on emerging social video spaces have panned out in the past. She pointed to Periscope, a now-defunct livestreaming service under Twitter, and BeReal, a pandemic-era flash in the pan. Still, Wen expressed optimism about microdramas, which have become a mainstream fixture in China and continue to see traction in the U.S., where mobile-first viewing is becoming more dominant.

“You realize that there is something here that people do engage with organically,” said Wen of microdramas.

“To me, it seems like it's worth learning in terms of how U.S. audiences — and then consumers of our brands — would adapt to that behavior here and make sure that we're on the forefront of that,” Wen added. “The only way that we know that is to actually engage with it and try a couple of different things.”

#7340. In graphic detail: Six thousand brands later, ChatGPT ads still have a conversion problem

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

ChatGPT now handles roughly a fifth of the search queries Google does. Its ads business is worth considerably less. Nobody should be surprised by that. OpenAI only started selling ads this year, the format they’re growing into is still taking shape and the company has been tiptoeing around how much advertising to put in front of users. Advertising is, in other words, lagging usage. OpenAI will want that to change quickly and advertisers are already testing how fast it can.

There’s room for OpenAI to increase the reach of its ad business within markets where it already operates. According to data from Similarweb and Graphite, the U.K. has the highest proportion of ad-eligible ChatGPT users seeing ads (45.7%), followed by the U.S. (43.4%) and Australia (43.3%).

But the data shows fewer than half of eligible users, even in OpenAI’s most established ad markets, are currently being reached by ads. In Mexico, for example, that figure falls to just 27.8%. The differences could reflect how far along each market is in the advertising rollout, or how much of its eligible audience OpenAI is currently monetizing.

The mix of advertisers spending on ChatGPT is also broadening beyond retail. According to data from Sensor Tower, shopping accounted for 38% of U.S. ad spend on the platform in April, but that share fell to 20% by September.

Over the same period, financial services increased its share of spend on ChatGPT from 2% to 13%, while software grew from 13% to 18%, and travel and tourism increased from 5% to 9%. This suggests that ChatGPT is attracting demand from a wider range of advertisers, rather than relying on a narrow range of verticals looking to reach consumers in shopping-related moments.

This has certainly broadened since the ad pilot launched in February. While Sensor Tower only started tracking ad spend in April 2026, at launch, the company estimates that around 42% of ads on ChatGPT were from advertisers in the shopping, followed by jobs & education (10%), food & dining (9%), software (8%), media and entertainment (8%) and travel (7%).

The number of brands advertising on ChatGPT is growing too. Data from Adthena shows that it tracked 1,082 distinct brands running ads on the platform, across the U.S., Australia, the U.K. and the EU in June. That number rose to 3,741 in July and 6,333 in August. And while the data for September only goes as far as Sept. 20, that full month is expected to reach around 6,830, based on Adthena’s data and projections.

It points to a growing pool of brands advertising on ChatGPT as the platform expands its markets and advertising capabilities.

New Street Research models the number of commercial chats on ChatGPT to grow from 45.9 billion this year, to nearly 380 billion by 2030, as the proportion of total chats carrying ads increases from 20% to 28% during the same period.

But the volume of ad opportunities is only part of the equation. The way those conversations are monetized will also matter. OpenAI recently said CPC and outcome-optimized bidding account for the majority of its ad campaigns, a higher proportion than New Street had previously assumed in its own model.

That could be positive for revenue, given CPC ads typically generate more per-ad event than impression-based pricing. However, New Street cautions that monetization may currently be weaker than it had originally modelled, suggesting its estimated CPC of around $5 could be too high.

That said, there is a question over whether ChatGPT ads are currently delivering enough value to advertisers to support the kind of revenue growth OpenAI is targeting. Research from a working paper under review by Maximilian Kaiser and Christian Schulze, based on data from ecommerce websites, found that paid ChatGPT visits generate $0.89 in revenue per session for U.S. advertisers, compared with $2.45 for paid Google Search visits.

The difference was also reflected in conversion rates: 0.37% for paid ChatGPT traffic, compared with 1.17% for Google. The findings suggest that while advertisers are increasingly testing the platform, OpenAI still has work to do to demonstrate that its ads can compete with established performance channels.

Those performance questions are compounded by ongoing measurement issues, according to Accuracast’s group CEO Farhad Divecha, who said the latest partner integrations — Magnite and Pubmatic — have not necessarily addressed the underlying tracking issues.

“We continue to see discrepancies there,” he said. “Some are teething problems because it’s a new system that has to be set up on advertiser systems, but some problems are down to attribution being messed up.”

Big names like actor Terry Crews and athletes could soon appear in AI-generated ads across the web. But they will be marked as such.

Advertisers now demand creator metrics like cost per lead and conversions, not follower counts.

The AI platform is testing an analytics dashboard that counts how often a business shows up in ChatGPT’s answers.

#7337. Why Liquid Death’s media chief is determined to break away from LiveRamp

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Liquid Death’s chief media officer could do without another branded hoodie this holiday season. A credible alternative to LiveRamp would go down far better.

Benoit Vatere has been looking for one since early August, when the beverage brand stopped the identity graph, after about 18 months of usage. The decision came weeks after Publicis Groupe’s plan to acquire LiveRamp was made public. In an interview with Digiday, Vatere said it was a simple choice: “I don’t want to train or have them see that super valuable information [for] anybody that I’m trying to get market share from.”

That fear stems from what LiveRamp learned about Liquid Death’s customers. The company’s job was to recognize the same people as they moved between screens and sites, so someone who saw an ad on a smart TV could be found again on a phone or a retailer’s site.

Over time that showed the brand which ads got people buying. Names and email addresses that get swept up in that tracking don’t concern Vatere since Publicis won’t see personal information. He’s far more concerned about the buying patterns. Publicis, after all, also works for brands Liquid Death competes with, like Evian and PepsiCo.

Vatere worries his patterns could end up in the data the holdco uses to train its AI tools and subsequently help make strategic calls for those rivals. Granted, the deal’s terms say that can’t happen. But Vatere expects them to change — terms and conditions get rewritten all the time, after all — not to mention the FTC’s watch over the deal lasts only five years.

“Let’s be clear about why Publicis bought LiveRamp,” continued Vatere. “They did it because there is value right? And the value is not just the revenue.”

That’s an indictment of every holdco with designs on an identity graph as it is of Publicis specifically. Vatere won’t hand his data to any company that also advises his competitors, which rules out the likes of Havas (still a LiveRamp user), WPP or Omnicom (which is building its own alternative).

In fact, Omnicom’s Acxiom has already tried its luck. Vatere sent it packing on the same grounds. “I’m not mad at Publicis. I just don’t want any of the holdcos to own it.”

That leaves independent providers, and Vatere isn’t naive about them either. Most are built with an acquisition and an exit in mind, so whichever one he picks will have to sign a contract that ends the relationship and deletes Liquid Death’s data the moment control changes hands.

“There should be a clause [that] if there’s change of ownership, change of control, contract out, right? Data deleted, like the whole thing, “ added Vatere.

He has a deadline of sorts. The LiveRamp contract runs out at the end of the year, and Liquid Death isn’t big enough to build its own graph. “I cannot do without it right now,” he said.

Chances are he won’t have to. The industry has no shortage of would-be LiveRamp replacements and plenty of them have already found their way to Vatere’s DMs. Earlier this week, the marketer posted his stance on LinkedIn after seeing reports that the holdco wasn’t concerned about competitors leaving now the deal has closed.

The replies read like a who’s who of the opportunists circling the fallout. One of them was LiveRamp’s CEO, said Vatere. He sent him a connection request, which was duly accepted. Nothing has come of it so far. Not much could. The only thing that would win him back is LiveRamp pulling out of the deal — and that ship has sailed.

Vatere is far from the only industry player to have taken this stance. LiveRamp rivals have been fielding calls and pitching for business since the deal was announced in the Spring. Both WPP and Omnicom have stopped working with the solution.

Advertisers have spent years investing heavily in cloud infrastructure, customer data platforms and first-party data infrastructure with the aim of retaining ownership of customer information as they activate media strategies. CMOs worried about maintaining that control may be at the front of the queue to speak to alternative providers.

“Advertising is an inherently zero-sum game. You need to beat your competitors to grow,” said Ruben Schreurs, CEO at Ebiquity. “Taking a uniform commoditized approach to communications or media is not a good strategy.”

“If your identity graph and your customer intelligence is your most important intellectual property, then you can’t choose infrastructure whose business model creates the appearance of conflicting incentives,” said Lai.

Schreurs argued, however, that working with LiveRamp alongside rivals won’t be the act that jeopardises a brand’s edge. “I do think it’s a little overplayed,” he said. “You can absolutely manage things in a way that if [your] agency also works for competing brands, that doesn’t necessarily have to be a problem. The same is true with LiveRamp.”

Whether any of this has a material impact on the trajectory of the business under Publicis remains uncertain. The likely answer is that Publicis wants LiveRamp’s data and connections for its own AI ambitions, combining LiveRamp’s clean room and data connectivity tech with the identity capabilities of Epsilon and the tech services of Publicis Sapient.

In any case, many of Vatere’s peers may find themselves boxed into to signing up with a Publicis-backed LiveRamp, or one of the rival holdco solutions, due to the market’s contraction in recent years.

“Clients absolutely care about conflict, how conflict is managed, but they have less and less optionality,” noted Schreurs. “There is now a big global three to choose from, so you’re going to work with a company that also works for competing brands. Then it becomes all about how you retain your competitive edge.”

Competitors and non-Publicis businesses are also a sliver of the business, accounting for roughly 5% of LiveRamp’s $800 million annual revenue.

A few defections at the edges are a price the holdco can afford to pay. Whether marketers like Vatere peel away in big enough numbers to change that sum remains an open question.

Forrester’s Wave study assessed 12 agency groups for their current offerings and strategies, and found diminishing differentiation among the largest of them.

Amazon’s shopping agent now takes orders by text message and the thread where those orders get placed will carry ads.

#7336. McDonald’s US CMO steps down as sales decline

As the fast-food giant searches for a new marketing leader, it will split the role between two longtime executives on an interim basis.

Alyssa Buetikofer, chief marketing and customer experience officer of McDonald’s USA, will leave the company next month to spend more time with her family, according to an internal note obtained by Marketing Dive. A 15-year veteran at McDonald’s, the executive was appointed to her latest role less than two years ago when Tariq Hassan stepped down.

As the company conducts a search for its new U.S. CMO, its marketing will be supported by two current executives in interim roles. Global CMO and Head of New Business Ventures Morgan Flatley will also serve as interim chief creative officer, while company veteran Charlie Newberger will serve as interim senior vice president, U.S. chief marketing and commercial officer.

The shakeup arrives a day after McDonald’s reported that U.S. comparable sales declined 0.2% in Q3 2026. However, global comparable sales increased 1.5% and the company beat Wall Street expectations.

Buetikofer will remain with McDonald’s through Dec. 18 to help support the leadership transition. The note cited her impact on McDonald’s business, where she recently oversaw the return of Snack Wraps and Monopoly, as well as the launch of a new beverage platform and a successful Grinch collaboration.

Flatley previously served as U.S. chief marketing and digital customer experience officer from 2017 to 2021 before taking on the global leadership role. She is described in the note as the driving force behind the brand’s “Fan Truths” strategy and the “Famous Orders” platform.

“In today’s environment, creativity is the difference maker for brands, and Morgan’s leadership puts us in a strong position to develop big ideas that build the brand and accelerate growth,” according to the note.

Newberger has been at McDonald’s for more than 17 years and was most recently corporate vice president and global category head for beverages and desserts. In the interim, he will lead marketing operations and work to translate strategy into commercial execution across the U.S. business.

The leadership moves and CMO search come as McDonald’s works to implement a strategic plan that it unveiled in June and detailed at a September investor day. Marketing is part of a consumer-focused pillar of the plan that includes creating brand-led content and co-creating with fans to reach consumers in relevant and personalized ways. McDonald’s last month also stepped away from the FIFA World Cup, a move that could disrupt the QSR sports sponsorship space.

#7333. How TF is the grocery store a media channel?

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This WTF guide, sponsored by Grocery TV, explores how marketers are reimagining in-store retail media to engage consumers inside the grocery store.

Even with the growth of digital channels such as e-commerce, social and CTV, the physical store remains one of the most influential moments in the path to purchase.

According to Grocery TV’s 2026 In-Store Shopper Perception Report, 95% of grocery shoppers make at least half of their purchase decisions in-store. Even younger, in-demand audiences frequent physical retail locations, with 55% of Gen Z consumers shopping in-store at least once a week.

Despite this, marketers have historically treated the store more like a retail backdrop than a media channel. Media buyers, especially digital media buyers, have long defaulted to other channels that were easier to tap into. For digital buyers accustomed to unified inventory, real-time optimization and closed-loop attribution, in-store was the opposite: a fragmented, retailer-by-retailer negotiation with no standardized metrics.

Now, in-store can be bought through a single platform, targeted with the precision of digital and measured across the funnel, making it possible to plan against the store with more sophistication.

Investment is following, with eMarketer projecting that U.S. in-store retail media spending will cross the billion-dollar milestone to reach $1.15 billion by 2029.

“We’re seeing advertisers lean in, testing and learning what works now that the infrastructure and measurement capabilities are in place,” said Marlow Nickell, co-founder and CEO of Grocery TV. “There’s a lot of opportunity ahead as more retailers launch their in-store networks.”

In this WTF explainer guide, Digiday and Grocery TV examine how savvy brands, retailers and agencies are leveraging the grocery store as a media channel. The majority of brand choice is made in a retail environment — not on TV or online — and now is the time for nonendemic and CPG marketers alike to rethink how they use the grocery store to engage audiences.

In-store grocery media uses digital screens placed throughout grocery stores to reach shoppers during their shopping trips.

The channel combines the strengths of three other established media channels:  the reach and context of DOOH, the storytelling capabilities of video and the audience and commerce connection of retail media. These qualities come together inside the physical grocery environment, reaching consumers during their routine grocery shopping trip.

Nickell sees proximity to the purchase decision as the key distinction. “With digital out-of-home, it’s often more on-the-go media, like a billboard you’re driving past. In-store retail media is much closer to the point of purchase, where shoppers are making decisions and considering different products,” said Nickell.

Chelsea Monaco, svp, commerce media, Digitas North America, noted that this blending of channels makes classification tricky. “In-store retail media today can be categorized multiple ways, and it varies by the organization,” she said.

“The intersection of digital and in-store blurs in the retail media space, and the investment sources are so unique,” Monaco said. “I’ve typically seen it most categorized as shopper marketing, but investment source and where it sits in the organization depict the true categorization.”

Grocery trips offer more opportunities for product discovery than checking items off a list might suggest. Shoppers can encounter new products, respond to promotions and make unplanned decisions as they browse the aisles.

According to Grocery TV’s In-Store Shopper Perception 2026 report, 32% of shoppers enter the store without a list, and 78% make at least one impulse purchase during their trip.

In-store media can influence those decisions. Grocery TV research found that 62% of shoppers have purchased something directly after seeing it on an in-store screen. Screens are also shoppers’ top way to learn about sales, promotions and new products, allowing brands to directly engage shoppers at the point of purchase.

“When you ask people what they like most about grocery shopping, usually the answer is the discovery. They like the fact that they can see and hold things, and really learn about new products,” Nickell said. “That’s why we find shoppers are open to advertising. They’re already in a buying mindset.”

That opportunity is only growing. In 2026, shopper receptivity increased across every major store zone compared to Grocery TV’s 2023 study. Digital display formats saw an average 10-percentage-point increase in positive customer experience ratings, climbing from 41% to 51%. This suggests that in-store media can be relevant to a broad range of grocery shoppers.

Grocery retailers with in-store media networks run digital screens throughout the store, concentrated in high-traffic and high-dwell spots like entrances and checkout where shoppers pass through or wait. Brands match their creative to the products and audiences they want to reach, such as a financial services brand connecting with shoppers as they wait at checkout.

“The screen is physically there in the environment,” Nickell said. “You don’t have to worry about an ad being blocked or scrolled out of view in the same way you do online.”

Where screens appear also matters. Research from Grocery TV shows that shoppers are more receptive to screens in high-dwell areas, where they naturally spend time waiting, such as the pharmacy, deli and checkout. Shoppers rated these zones as 84% favorable, according to Grocery TV’s 2026 In-Store Shopper Perception Report.

Entrances are another opportunity, as shoppers are accustomed to seeing signage there. By contrast, placements that interrupt the shopping experience, such as cooler screens, tend to be less popular.

Effective in-store creative reflects both the product being advertised and the context in which shoppers encounter it. Brands can tailor messaging to the shopping environment, seasonal needs or specific audiences.

Nickell encourages brands to advertise across the entire store, with creative that connects to the shopping environment and gives shoppers a reason to engage beyond their planned purchases.

For instance, a snack brand might build a campaign around a game-day occasion, using creative that inspires shoppers to add something to their basket whether they see it entering the store or waiting at checkout. Education-forward content, such as recipes or tips for choosing an insurance carrier, can also give shoppers something useful to engage with during their visit.

Shoppers are 2.5 times more likely to consider a brand when an ad is contextually relevant to its environment, according to research from Grocery TV. The surrounding context can also shape how shoppers perceive an ad: 84% viewed end-cap ads featuring contextually relevant creative favorably or neutrally, compared with 79% for ads that were unrelated to the environment.

“As an industry, we often focus on the capability or the creative, but we forget about the overall experience,” Monaco said. “The biggest miss that I’ve seen is not connecting the entire shopper journey or that experience in how that shopper is going to engage with your brand in-store. What experience do you want that shopper to have with your brand at that touchpoint, and how does that fit in with all of the other omnichannel touchpoints they might experience?”

Brands getting started can adapt existing creative from channels such as CTV or social, making adjustments for the in-store environment. Text should be easy to read from a distance, and branding should be prominent. Over time, brands can develop creative specifically for in-store placements, incorporating context such as location, retailer and time of day.

As in-store media has evolved, audience targeting has become more sophisticated, bringing it closer to the strategies marketers already use in TV and digital campaigns. By combining geographic, demographic and behavioral signals with the context of the physical store, advertisers can reach shoppers close to the point of purchase with greater precision.

Geographic targeting can narrow a campaign by state, designated market area (DMA), ZIP code or point of interest. A brand looking to reach students and other campus communities, for example, could target grocery stores near universities.

Behavioral signals, such as purchase patterns and responsiveness to promotions, can help brands refine their audiences further. Demographic signals like age, income and household composition offer another layer. Brands can also prioritize stores that over-index for specific shopper groups, such as Hispanic consumers or older adults.

For example, a telecom brand promoted its international access plan across Grocery TV’s retailer-owned network in stores that over-index for Hispanic shoppers. The campaign drove a 27% lift in consideration for the brand when shopping for a wireless plan, a 15% lift in purchase intent and a 12% lift in unaided brand awareness.

As in-store media becomes easier to plan and buy, marketers have more ways to evaluate its impact, from brand awareness and consideration to incremental sales and repeat purchase. Yet measurement hasn’t always kept pace with the range of outcomes the channel can influence. A 2026 Grocery TV and IAB study found that, across four of five key outcomes, more marketers believe in-store media can drive those outcomes than they can currently measure those outcomes.

Part of the challenge is operational. Brands can struggle to align internal KPIs and standardize measurement across campaigns, while ownership of in-store media still sits largely with shopper marketing teams. The marketers getting the most from in-store take a broader approach, measuring beyond immediate sales and involving national media and brand teams.

Measurement should start with the campaign objective, using tailored methodologies to track different outcomes:

Together, these approaches help advertisers assess performance across the funnel, using methods suited to the outcome they want to understand.

For example, a Grocery TV campaign for a financial health app measured brand impact against a control group, finding an 82% lift in brand rating, a 72% lift in recommendation and a 34% lift in brand awareness. The results show how in-store campaigns can be evaluated against objectives beyond immediate product sales.

“For banking, it makes a lot of sense to run ads at checkout. That’s when you’re pulling cards out of your wallet and making what, for most people, is the largest purchase of the week. Personal finances are already top of mind,” Nickell said.

Sales can be measured, too. In a campaign for Frito-Lay, Hy-Vee’s RedMedia and Grocery TV used a matched-store study across 103 stores, comparing test and control groups over a month. The campaign generated a 15% sales lift and a $7.94 return on ad spend.

Transaction-based measurement can also reveal effects beyond the advertised product. One national personal care brand working with Grocery TV saw a 15% sales lift for the advertised product, a 12% lift across complementary products and an 8% lift across its full brand portfolio.

The opportunity is to measure outcomes aligned with the campaign’s goals, including what happens after the initial purchase. In the IAB study, 75% of marketers who said they fully leverage in-store media measured post-purchase outcomes such as loyalty and repeat purchase, compared with 42% of those who said they underutilize the channel. Measuring beyond immediate sales can give marketers a fuller picture of the role in-store media plays in driving customer retention.

The grocery store can play a complementary role in an integrated plan, connecting brand messaging across channels with the moment shoppers are making purchase decisions. It starts with a clear business objective: What does the campaign need to achieve, and where could in-store contribute?

That may mean reconsidering how budgets are allocated across channels. “The ultimate question is what business outcome will drive the most success,” Monaco said. “We’ve seen cases where it might make sense to take budget out of underperforming digital and move it to in-store.”

A study from Grocery TV and MFour found that in-store media delivers an average of 49% incremental reach beyond existing media plans. Complementing campaigns on other channels with in-store media allows marketers to reduce overexposure by diversifying audiences. They can also maximize media dollars by driving new engagement and closing gaps left by other channels, such as display and online video.

Marketers don’t need to rethink their entire media mix to explore the channel, however. They can start with a defined objective, test in-store alongside other media and evaluate its incremental contribution to outcomes like brand lift or sales. Those findings can help determine where future investment will have the greatest impact.

In-store retail media is still developing as a full-funnel media-buying channel, giving early adopters an edge before it becomes a standard part of media planning. As Nickell noted, brands that start testing now can learn which creative works, how to measure success and how to make the case for investment internally. Those learnings take time to build. “The earlier you get started, the better,” Nickell said.

Big names like actor Terry Crews and athletes could soon appear in AI-generated ads across the web. But they will be marked as such.

Google is moving on two fronts: working up the U.K’s Competition and Markets Authority-mandated search opt-out publishers have long sought, while enhancing AI Overviews in ways that could make opting out less appealing.

Advertisers now demand creator metrics like cost per lead and conversions, not follower counts.
