#7311. Media Buying Briefing: Forrester’s Wave report finds growing homogeneity among the 12 biggest agencies

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Media Buying Briefing covers the latest in agency news and media buying for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Monday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

The latest iteration of Forrester’s Wave Report on media agencies — rather, media management services, as the research firm puts it — is out, assessing the 12 biggest agency groups currently operating today that are of interest to Forrester’s clients. And guess what? Consolidation has made the biggest of them look more alike than ever.

“I was anticipating more differentiation in the marketplace as a result of of the consolidations and acquisitions that took place,” said Pattisall, referring to things like Omnicom’s acquisition of IPG and Publicis’ various tech and segment purchases, as well as PE-backed acquisitions like Wpromote’s purchase of Giant Spoon. “I was anticipating that we would see more separation, if you will. Once you look outside of scale and size, then the differences are much more subtle.”

To be clear, there are winners and losers as the report lays out in an opening Venn diagram. Three firms were listed as “leaders,” according to a scoring system based on 19 criteria ranging from “agentic media buying” to “customer lifecycle management” and “global delivery strategy”. They were, of course, the Big Three (Omnicom, Publicis and WPP). Dentsu and Havas led the “strong performers” group, along with Wpromote, PMG, Tinuiti and Horizon Media. Filling out the “contenders” category were Stagwell, Dept and Rise (a Quad agency).

Private-equity-backed shops like Wpromote fared well, and Forrester’s description gave it props for pursuing agentic media buying as well as AEO abilities that “unites paid, owned, and earned authority-building with the measurement of visibility, sentiment, and traffic quality.”

“We valued their measurement capabilities,” Pattisall said. “We valued their precision capabilities. We valued their planning acumen, their optimization mindset — all of that plays very well for that group. It is the reason that you see relatively smaller organizations in the ‘strong performer’ grouping.”

Reacting to its position as the highest-ranking independent in terms of strength of offering in the report, Wpromote CEO Andrea Bendzick cited the big opportunity for independents.

“We’re at a point where the industry is being reshaped by AI, new ways of reaching consumers and undeniable pressure to connect marketing investments to business outcomes,” said Bendzick. “Independent agencies are well positioned to invest in those emerging areas and build their businesses around where clients are going, not where they’ve been.”

Other reaction to the report was mixed, with Publicis demurring from any comment (it was the only agency group that declined to participate in the report’s outreach for insights and explanations, as well as client-side input). With its run of successful client wins and poaches, the argument can be made that it doesn’t need to go out there and prove itself to prospective clients through a Forrester report, but it also is perceived as arrogant by some in the industry.

Regardless, Forrester’s scoring credits the holdco’s media buying, principal media, and data and marketing sciences capabilities — and awarded Publicis the highest average score in strategy, a 4.4 out of 5. “These strengths plus its impressive media buying scale produce exceptional client media value. Customer lifecycle management also excels, with global client leads coordinating resource access and keeping implementation teams involved after launch,” reads the report. However, of the Big Three, it also scored the only 1s (the lowest score) in “agentic media buying” due to a lack of “tangible pilots, scaled partnerships, or evidence of meaningful client impact, and SEO/AEO which “lacks proactive experimentation.”

Omnicom declared validation for its acquisition of IPG — and it received the highest score in “current offering,” at a 3.8 out of 5 — which Forrester credited it for being the largest buyer both domestically and globally. The holdco wasted no time in celebrating its solid ranking as a “leader”.

“In a marketplace being reshaped by AI, commerce and increasingly complex consumer journeys, we believe Forrester offers marketers a valuable perspective on what truly differentiates a leader: the ability to connect intelligence, technology, partnerships and buying power to deliver measurable business value,” said Omnicom Media CEO Florian Adamski in a prepared statement.

Regarding WPP, Pattisall noted that “their capabilities in the media marketplace exceed their perception in the marketplace,” due to the fact that Wall Street is still punishing the British-owned holdco for the sins of prior management. “What their stock price is isn’t going to drive their value to a client, and we’re measuring the latter and not the former,” he added.

“What’s increasingly clear is that competitive advantage in marketing will come not from who owns or accumulates the most data but from who can unlock the most intelligence from it and put that intelligence to work,” said Lauren Wetzel, global president, data and technology solutions at WPP.

Interestingly, Stagwell, which many do not consider a holding company — scored somewhat poorly in the Wave report, averaging a 2.4 in strategy and a 2.44 in current offering. Only Rise (scoring a 1.6 in strategy) and Dept (a 2.2 in strategy) scoring lower averages.

Pattisall in part attributed Stagwell’s lower score to “the newness of the Stagwell Media strategy,” which he credited with being “a very calculated and appropriate move to put together compatible capabilities to go to market at the group level, if if and when necessary.”

But it got dinged, he added, because “they are a relatively new organization. Their mission and their vision and their position in the marketplace is not as sharp as the others because they have not been operating and functioning at this level but for only a couple of months.”

Missing from the list because they either didn’t meet the $3.1 billion billings threshold or because Forrester clients weren’t as interested in them were S4 Capital’s Monks and other independents including Crossmedia or Brainlabs.

With midterm elections finally over — and one can once again watch Jeopardy without the constant bombardment of negative political ads — estimates for total  political ad spending vary from a low of $11.4 billion by AdImpact, which is seen as the most reliable figure, to OpenSecret’s $11.9 billion, which it said it based on an analysis of Federal Election Commission disclosure data. Either way it’s a record — and it seems to indicate that the 2028 presidential election cycle will raise the bar even higher. Whether candidates find other ways to spend their money besides 30-second TV spots remains to be seen.

“The question CMOs are asking isn’t whether LiveRamp still works. It’s whether they want their customer data running through infrastructure owned by an agency holding company that competes with their other agencies. A neutrality pledge is a promise; neutrality by design is an architecture. More brands want their identity and audience data to stay in their own warehouse, under their own control, so they can work with any agency or partner without routing everything through one company’s pipes. The deal didn’t create that shift, but it has definitely sped it up.”

Amazon’s shopping agent now takes orders by text message and the thread where those orders get placed will carry ads.

A recap of the sessions and insights coming out of Digiday’s Fall Media Buying Summit, which centered on AI’s impact on the media agency world.

In the wake of LiveRamp’s acquisition by Publicis, some marketers are auditioning alternatives to the identity graph company.

#7310. Pepsi’s holiday push includes bigger retail presence, soda for Santa

New ads expand on a “Food Deserves Pepsi” platform that launched in 2024 and positions the soft drink as the ideal pairing with food.

Pepsi is ramping up its marketing activity around the holiday and football seasons, two occasions with a strong connection to food, for efforts that build on a “Food Deserves Pepsi” platform that launched in 2024 and positions the soft drinks as an essential pairing with food. Other moments Pepsi has looked to plug itself into as part of the platform have included soccer viewing parties, barbecues and pizza delivery.

The TV spot will be amplified across a variety of channels and during key moments, including NBC’s holiday tree lighting programming, college football games on Christmas Eve and Christmas Day NBA and NFL games. Additionally, Pepsi expects to have 80% more visibility in retail locations over the year-ago period through holiday packaging, displays, inventory and customer-specific programming throughout the season.

A limited-edition Pepsi Vanilla & Cream Holiday Bundle will be available starting Nov. 19 on Walmart’s website and includes holiday baking items and Pepsi merchandise. Pepsi Vanilla & Cream launched Oct. 18 and has so far received a strong consumer reception, per the brand.

Tapping into football hype, Pepsi launched custom creative featuring Bettis on Nov. 4 across Walmart’s website and social channels. A video, titled “Flag on Flavor,” sees the NFL legend surprising Walmart shoppers while promoting a “Tailgating Deserves Pepsi” campaign. The effort will also be promoted across 15 NFL team partnerships via retail, new creative and in-person experiences.

Pepsi has deepened its connection to football via retail visibility, with a 30% increase in point-of-sale presence over last year, per the brand. Pepsi has been the official soft drink of the NFL for nearly three decades. Earlier this year, the brand advertised in the Super Bowl with a 30-second national spot that featured a polar bear who is befuddled after picking Pepsi Zero Sugar over Coke Zero in a blind taste test.

PepsiCo in Q3 saw net revenue increase 5.6%, per an earnings statement. For its beverages division, the company reported improving organic volume trends, driven by strength in energy drinks, functional hydration and zero-sugar and flavored soft drinks. Still, PepsiCo is looking to trim spending as its broader North American business continues to face sales challenges amid shifting consumer habits.

This fall, KFC U.S. announced that it will replace Pepsi beverages with Coca-Cola across its restaurant system by spring 2027. The win for rival Coke comes as its Mr. Pibb beverage rises as the fastest-growing brand this year. Another rival, Keurig Dr Pepper, is planning to increase its marketing investment, potentially presenting another challenge for PepsiCo.

#7309. Claims Manager at Counterpart

Counterpart is an insurtech platform reimagining management and professional liability for the modern workplace. We believe that when businesses lead with clarity and confidence, they become more resilient, more innovative, and better prepared for what’s ahead. That’s why we built the first Agentic Insurance™ system — where advanced AI and deep insurance expertise come together to proactively assess, mitigate, and manage risk. Backed by A rated carriers and trusted by brokers nationwide, our platform helps small businesses grow with confidence. Join us in shaping a smarter future, helping businesses Do More With Less Risk®.

As a member of the Claims team, you will be one of the initial users of, and key feedback providers for, our active claims management processes and systems. Your input will help to shape and improve how we fulfill our mission of providing world-class service through tightly managing legal costs, making data-driven decisions when analyzing a claim’s value, and ensuring that other potentially responsible parties pay their fair share. This will necessitate close collaboration with Claims leadership, our internal Data and Engineering teams, and our carrier partners.

#7308. WTF is the AI training opt-out policy token?

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is a WTF explainer, in which we break down media and marketing’s most confusing terms. More from the series →

Some of the largest AI tech companies — including Anthropic, Google, Microsoft and OpenAI — have agreed to support a new mechanism that lets publishers opt out of having their content used to train AI models, in just a few lines of text.

The proposed standard, called a “policy token” for AI training, comes as publishers seek more control over how tech companies scrape and use their content. They’ve tried blocking AI bots, pursuing legal action and negotiating licensing deals.

At first glance, this AI training policy token appears to offer a straightforward fix. In reality, it highlights why the problem is so complicated.

The idea is that publishers can add a line to their robots.txt file — which websites use to tell web crawlers what content they are allowed to access — to express whether they want their content used for AI training.

Here’s what the policy token looks like, as written in robots.txt: User-Agent: __usage_ai-training

Below that, publishers can write “disallow” or “allow,” to show which crawlers can scrape and which can’t. In the “allow” line, publishers can also offer certain exceptions, such as letting crawlers scrape a press kit.

Typically, publishers need to list each crawler individually in robots.txt and give permission (or not) to crawl their sites. The new AI training policy token would allow publishers to do so in one step. Instead of managing separate instructions for every crawler, they can set broad preferences to participating AI and tech companies.

The proposal was written by Kevin Kelley, a technical lead for standards at Anthropic. Kelley submitted the proposal to the Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF), an internet standards development organization, on Oct. 30.

Technically, the standard is not official yet. The IETF launched an effort known as AI Preferences (AIPREF) to develop new standards for website owners to express preferences about how their content gets used for AI. Anthropic, Google, Microsoft and OpenAI have all been involved in the group.

While the largest search and AI providers have agreed to support this policy token, it still needs to be deliberated on by the IETF. And the fact that these major companies have jumped to support this draft raises some concerns, said Alex Springer, technical lead at SPUR, a coalition of publishers working to develop AI standards.

“The IETF exists to allow for individuals to work in public to form consensus about the standards and protocols that make the internet work. The way that the platforms have approached this by announcing their unilateral support of a ‘standard’ that is an unreviewed draft is the antithesis of that,” he said. “They appear to have decided to unilaterally wield their collective weight in a concerted effort to force a new de facto standard in place, one that falls far short of what those content owners require.”

Anthropic says it is rolling out adoption of the signal now.

In a blog post, OpenAI said it would honor the policy token “in the coming months.” It noted that the proposed mechanism would not replace its existing crawler controls.

Google wrote in its own blog post that it aims to support this policy token in 2027. It noted that it builds on the options it already offers publishers, such as Google-Extended, a crawler that publishers can block in robots.txt to control AI training access, implemented in 2023.

Microsoft went a bit further, writing in a blog post that not only would it comply with the AI training policy token but that it would also offer publishers the ability to opt out of AI grounding, or when crawlers scrape sites to find real-time information. These preferences will replace the existing Microsoft preferences “NoArchive” and “NoCache,” for AI training and grounding respectively, starting Feb. 1, 2027.

Meta didn’t go as far as to say it would honor the policy token, but Andy Stone, a Meta company spokesperson, wrote on X: “Meta, too, strongly support publishers’ ability to express preferences about how their content is used for foundational generative AI training. We believe creating a common signal that conveys such preferences to multiple providers at once is an important step. And we look forward to working with other AI companies on the standards and their implementation across the industry.”

The proposal may appeal to publishers seeking standardized controls. But, like robots.txt, it offers no way to ensure AI and tech companies comply with publishers’ preferences.

Robots.txt preferences are infamously more of a suggestion than an enforceable doctrine.

In fact, data suggests that web crawlers rarely even check publishers’ robots.txt files when scraping their sites. Crawlers operated by the four companies backing the shared opt-out token made roughly 1 billion requests over the past 30 days — but just 0.15% of those requests were for robots.txt files, according to data shared with Digiday by AI visibility measurement firm ScalePost, which tracked activity across more than 50 publisher properties.

Paul Bannister, chief strategy officer at Raptive, called the AI training policy token “window dressing.”

“I think this is useless and is marketing, and far, far, far too late… It does nothing and doesn’t solve the problem at all,” he said.

AI tech companies have a lot of reasons to want to appear to appease publishers. They’re fighting a number of copyright infringement lawsuits, pressure from government regulators, and proposed legislation targeting crawlers.

Four major AI tech companies have said they will honor this AI training policy token, making this one of the first proposed standards with that kind of support.

While it remains to be seen if this will be the case in practice, the policy token could be another layer publishers have at their disposal to protect their content from web crawlers, in addition to mechanisms like blocking crawlers at the CDN level or using tools like TollBit.

“Even though it doesn’t keep all the thieves out, you’d still put a lock on your front door because it keeps some of the thieves out… You do lots and lots of things to minimize the chances that your house gets broken into, but you cannot prevent it completely,” Bannister said. “Is it one other arrow to put in your quiver of about 1,000 things you need to do? Sure.”

Many publishers have been working hard to improve their AI visibility, and some are selling GEO products to brands to boost their visibility in LLMs. The last thing those publishers want is to jeopardize their mentions and citations in those tools.

Of the four companies backing the policy token, Microsoft was the only one to explicitly address its implications for AI visibility. If a publisher does disallow crawlers for AI grounding, “you will see reduced brand visibility in AI experiences,” reads Microsoft’s blog post.

This is part of the reason publishers are wary of some of Google’s AI opt-out mechanisms, which let publishers exclude their content from AI-powered search features but could mean sacrificing valuable visibility and traffic they can’t afford to lose.

The AI training policy token doesn’t have anything to do with AI inference (or AI usage), which is when an AI model generates responses or performs tasks by retrieving or processing content from publishers’ websites in real time. This has become core to most of the recent AI licensing deals between publishers and tech companies.

And even if an AI crawler abides by publishers’ preferences expressed in robots.txt, it doesn’t mean the AI provider won’t be able to get that data elsewhere, such as from other crawlers, Bannister said.

“None of them have said, ‘We won’t buy data from third parties that scrape the content that don’t obey this,’ or ‘We won’t take open source crawls like Common Crawl,’ Bannister said. “They’re going to get all the data anyway, just through a different mechanism, and so it’s completely useless.”

The draft also appears to reinforce the idea that AI companies can use publishers’ content for training by default, unless publishers explicitly tell them not to, Springer said. This “opt in unless opted out” concept risks legitimizing a system where crawlers can presume they have permission for AI training, rather than needing explicit consent, he argued.

“The platforms are taking the stance that unless a preference to the contrary is expressed then content is fair game for AI training. We should not be normalizing this view and need to continue to work to maintain the concept that the usage of content should be determined by its owners and the result of their direct permission,” Springer said.

Google is moving on two fronts: working up the U.K’s Competition and Markets Authority-mandated search opt-out publishers have long sought, while enhancing AI Overviews in ways that could make opting out less appealing.

Instagram wants to pull long-form creators away from YouTube, but is it offering enough? Experts weigh in on the platform’s pivot.

Ad tech’s winners and losers emerge as CTV growth masks open-web struggles.

#7305. How performance metrics are rewriting creator rate cards

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Any creator asking for media dollars better have the reporting to back up the request. Advertisers aren’t cutting checks just on follower count and virality anymore.

To ink a creator deal, advertisers are asking for cost per lead, cost per conversion, earned media value and other performance metrics. In some cases, some brands are asking for Instagram link clicks and engaged YouTube views up front to determine if a creator is worth the ad spend.

“They want to see if there’s actual traffic being driven. We’re seeing a stronger emphasis on accountability for sales for each creator,” said Kimberly Duman, a managing partner at Glossary Artist creator management firm.

Creator campaigns are more often being judged against traditional performance marketing math: cost per conversion, cost per lead and return on ad spend.

Consider it yet another signal of the creator economy mirroring programmatic advertising.

As the tech has gotten better, creator platforms and agencies alike have spent the last year rolling out AI-powered dashboards with years of historical performance data. These souped up control panels allow brands to see a creator’s engagement rate, follower count, conversion rate and other performance stats before a deal is ever made.

Now, follower count becomes a vanity metric as brands look for metrics that prove purchasing behavior.

“Cost per lead or cost per conversion. Simple as that,” said Joseph Perello, founder and CEO of Props, a performance-based creator marketing platform, later adding, “Every client has a cost per conversion benchmark.”

Naturally, that benchmark looks different for every client. But as the tech improves, advertisers are retooling their scorecard to determine how good of a salesperson a creator is, he said.

Or as Stefani Stamatiou, CEO of Americas at HYDP, sees it: “The way that we work with a lot of our creators is to look at the performance of their content before we contract them.”

Before deal talks start, the global creator and influencer agency HYDP evaluates a creator’s content within a 30-day window, including view counts, but excludes any viral content to set reasonable expectations, Stamatiou said.

View counts has its own set of challenges. YouTube overhauled its view count in August. Rather than a count metric starting at several seconds in, a video view is registered the moment a video starts to play.

Now, advertiseres want “engaged views” — at least Blue Hour influencer agency is asking for those stats up front, Matt Higgins, svp and head of strategy at Blue Hour told Digiday.

“The bigger point is what we should focus on when finding and negotiating with creators,” Higgins said of the YouTube changes. “We negotiate around audience fit, shares, and expected performance, with views as one input.”

The brand tracks clicks and sales, but places greater emphasis on creator-brand alignment. “Great content will always win,” Glossary’s Duman said.

Big names like actor Terry Crews and athletes could soon appear in AI-generated ads across the web. But they will be marked as such.

The AI platform is testing an analytics dashboard that counts how often a business shows up in ChatGPT’s answers.

Despite leaning on creators for most of the year, marketers aren’t doing it for the holiday crunch. That job still belongs to celebrities.

#7301. As publishers await click data, Google’s AI visibility push complicates the opt-out decision

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Google’s latest publisher carrot? AI visibility.

Google is moving on two fronts: working up the U.K’s Competition and Markets Authority-mandated search opt-out publishers have long sought, while enhancing AI Overviews in ways that could make opting out less appealing.

The opt-out is a Search Console control that lets publishers exclude their content from Google’s AI features. The control follows the CMA’s first conduct requirement on Google, imposed over the summer. While the ruling was U.K.-only, Google opened it to sites worldwide on August 31. Page-level grounding controls are due by March 3, 2027.

But the controls are bundled: publishers can’t opt out of individual features. That leaves them weighing the potential visibility against the loss of control and waiting for click data to help judge the trade-off.

That click data, which Google is required to provide to U.K. publishers by Dec. 3, will give publishers a key missing piece of the puzzle. And given Google made the first requirements global, publishers hope it will provide click data globally too. Google hasn’t confirmed that.

Google has shown regulators and publishers that it is cooperating by sharing impression data: how often publisher content appears in AI features. But it has held back the click data, which would show whether those appearances actually send people to publishers’ sites. “That is the metric that will determine whether they [publishers] turn it off or not,” said Chris Dicker, CEO of Candr Media, which owns Trusted Reviews. “They’re not giving the metrics [currently] that actually mean anything, when they could have — because they’ve got those — they could have done it at the same time as impressions, but they haven’t.”

He added that “everyone is holding off” playing around with the new controls until they see the click data because they don’t want to turn off a large proportion of their activities.

Dicker expects Google to share the click data in the coming weeks, ahead of Black Friday, when publishers are unlikely to risk experimenting with opt-outs during a crucial sales period.

Meanwhile, Google has updated AI Mode and AI Overviews to show links more often and more prominently, added timely article carousels, and included links to related stories and more context around surfaced articles.

Google says these changes will help publishers better track, control and increase their content visibility. It’s also testing links from video creators’ and publishers’ names to their Search profiles, and says that its Preferred Sources — the feature that lets users pick and bookmark the specific websites and publishers they most trust — has grown sevenfold this year.

That visibility comes with a catch, though: the more valuable Google’s AI features become, the harder it may be to justify opting out.

For some publishers, that trade-off is already a live question. “We’re discussing the CMA controls and how we exercise those,” said Carly Steven, director of ecommerce and commercial editorial at the Daily Mail, at Digiday’s Media Strategies London event in October.

“We need to be so, so careful because just as we’re about to get the ability to remove articles on a URL basis, Google announces this new feature, and we have to ask ourselves: do we really not want to be there? It does still matter that we’re on the surfaces where users are. So it’s a very complicated picture.”

David Buttle, founder of publisher AI standards coalition SPUR, believes Google’s preferred approach is to provide enough referral traffic from its AI-related surfaces for publishers to agree to let Google use their content, rather than paying them each time it does so. That means it needs to continue providing some kind of value exchange. “The way they can achieve that within the context of the conduct requirement is to ensure they can deliver enough value in the form of traffic from those surfaces back to publishers, so that they can say yes voluntarily,” said Buttle.

Trying to maintain its exchange with publishers is tricky when its newest rival for information retrieval – OpenAI – isn’t constrained by the same legacy relationship. “ChatGPT doesn’t pay for content generally, and it doesn’t send any traffic out,” he added. “So Google’s approach is to try and intermediate as many clicks from Discover, which is a really important traffic source for publishers, and they’re serving summaries really aggressively in Discover, and bundling the control for that with the control for summaries everywhere else.”

For all the noise around opting out, publishers are unlikely to do so unless they can opt out in a more granular way, stressed Buttle. “What it [Google] can do, though, is play around with the interface and the product for Discover to juice the AI-intermediated referrals that come out of that to get as much traffic out of that, so if you’re a publisher, it makes that decision to opt out far harder,” he added.

For some publishers, the question is still how to assess the controls before deciding whether to use them. At The Economist Group, that process is only beginning.

“On the CMA point, we’re looking at it,” said Josh Muncke, vp of generative AI at The Economist Group on stage at the Digiday Media Strategies event earlier this month. “I don’t believe we’ve tested it yet, but we’re certainly looking at it.”

Whatever that decision is, it will ultimately hinge on two things: one is how much traffic Google sends The Economist. The other is how often its name appears in the AI answers at the top of the results, even when nobody clicks through. He added: There are second-order effects to turning these things off that aren’t just about the traffic. They’re about broader visibility in the world.”

Some of those effects are already on the table. Some publishers expect excluded sites to drop out of the Top Stories carousel Google now places inside AI Overviews and Google hasn’t said how its control treats that unit.

Reuters Digital Phil Andraos told Digiday in June that the lack of click data meant any decision could be slow and probably involve testing.

Muncke’s instinct runs the same way. He said he wouldn’t frame it as a single in-or-out call at a fixed point. “It will be a case more of thinking about where and how we can test, measure, and see the impacts, and make kind of like more structured, data-driven calls as we go,” he said.

The Professional Publishers Association (PPA) is appealing to the CMA to press Google to unbundle the controls so they apply to individual features, rather than solely site- and page-level, so that publishers can have a deeper level of control.

For example, a specific query answered by an AI Overview may replace a click a publisher would previously have received, while the same content could still be valuable in a broader, conversational AI search.

“You’re either opting out of search AI Mode, AI Overviews – basically all these different features, or none of them,” said PPA’s head of policy and Public Affairs Eilidh Wilson. “And this is something that we continue to hear is what most concerns publishers. We’ve been really wanting to land this with impact with the CMA, so we’ve agreed to do a further follow-up briefing with them on it to look at the different parts,” she added.

Major AI companies are backing a new AI training opt-out standard proposal for publishers, but it exposes deeper tensions over AI scraping.

Instagram wants to pull long-form creators away from YouTube, but is it offering enough? Experts weigh in on the platform’s pivot.

Ad tech’s winners and losers emerge as CTV growth masks open-web struggles.

#7300. Ogilvy and CAA launch partnership to clone celebrity talent for AI ads

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Body doubles are a popular subject in horror cinema. Usually, the doppelganger is the villain of the piece. But now, one of the leading gatekeepers in Hollywood is set to begin working with creative agency Ogilvy to run brand campaigns featuring entirely AI-generated celebrity appearances.

The WPP agency has inked a partnership with Creative Artists Agency (CAA) to generate AI ads featuring the actors, athletes and high-profile creators on CAA’s roster.

“We are witnessing the ‘NIL moment’ between advertising and entertainment,” said Carol Reed, global chief innovation officer at Ogilvy, referencing the Name, Image and Likeness industry that has grown around college sports influencers in the U.S. in recent years. “We’ve created a system where the biggest stars can license verified digital replicas safely… unlocking unprecedented agility for brands and assured control for talent.”

Brooklyn Nine-Nine star Terry Crews is among the actors who have opted in. Reed declined to name clients using the system, but said several campaigns were already being planned. “We are in the process of negotiating a few pilots right now that should be going live towards the end of the year,” she said.

The solution, called Echo, gives Ogilvy access to material kept in “The Vault” an in-house digital scanning facility built for CAA by tech company Veritone four years ago. Ogilvy and CAA’s partnership was a year in the making, per Reed. The studio uses 204 cameras to capture every facet of an actor, athlete or creator — from their voice to body language, facial expressions and the way they walk.

That material, which is refreshed by CAA clients every 3-5 years, is already used for de-aging or post-production fixes in films. Now, the agency’s staffers can pull it into WPP Open, the system behind the holding company’s various endeavors in creative production, agentic media buying and self-service creative tools, for internal storyboarding, mock-ups or final creative assets.

While the negotiations between actors or creators, their representatives and Ogilvy’s clients will still play out the same as always, the partnership is expected to yield more assets than classic shoots, at a faster pasce, and reducing cost-per-asset by 65-75%.

The combination of digital capture and AI content generation could “fundamentally change celebrity endorsement economics,” according to Gartner analyst Nicole Greene.

David Raichman, executive creative director and global AI creative lead at Ogilvy, called the system “the next frontier of content… more of it, faster, and – for the first time – more trusted, not less.”

If that sounds outlandish, consider the campaigns launched by Coca-Cola and Snickers earlier this year featuring soccer coach and CAA client José Mourinho — or rather, his digital clone. Ogilvy’s partnership with the agency, which Reed said could stretch to include other talent companies in the future, gives it the chance to scale up such projects.

Echo isn’t Ogilvy’s first effort in this realm. Five years ago, Ogilvy used digital scans of Bollywood star Shah Rukh Khan to advertise small businesses across India during the Hindu festival of Diwali. That project served as a kind of “prototype” for Echo, according to Reed.

She said the agency expects the partnership to be particularly useful for ads featuring sports personalities. “They’re locked into training and seasons, so there’s long periods where they’re not available,” she said. “Now they can kind of engage and work throughout a season where normally they wouldn’t be available.”

AI tools have become increasingly common in agency and in-house creative production efforts, though they’re typically deployed with performance media assets in mind — ads for digital display, paid social or mobile app environments — rather than campaign materials featuring a famous face. Though Reed said the agency expects to use the system initially for social assets, Ogilvy’s partnership gives it an edge for clients looking to take advantage of AI gains across the entire spectrum of advertising production.

“Agencies need a first-mover advantage right now,” said Greene.

For CAA, it’s a chance to “grow the pie,” according to Alex Shannon, the talent agency’s head of strategic development. Stars can take on commercial work unfettered by the schedule of their day job on the pitch or in the movie studio — allowing them to work with more brands overall, but also capitalize on moments when their profile might be highest, such as the climax to a sporting season or during the launch of a film or TV show.

“Now, brands have the ability to move at the speed of culture, and athletes are able to do even more to engage fans at times when that relevancy is so critically important,” she said.

The partnership between Ogilvy and CAA includes a framework designed to govern how and when the agency’s clients can use the likeness of talent. CAA clients own their digital likenesses, and are able to pick and choose which businesses and markets they’re happy to appear in ads for or formally endorse.

“Talent always gets to see everything and approve everything at the end,” said Reed. AI advertising materials will also include a watermark that clearly marks a video or image as both AI-generated and approved by the star in question. Reed said the measure is a means of reassuring consumers that “this isn’t a deep fake”, as well as staying ahead of future AI regulation. (The European Union, for example, has mandated gen AI providers put watermarks on content produced using their tools since August.)

CAA has been aggressive in defending the value of its clients’ likenesses, signing an agreement with YouTube to remove celeb deep fakes from the video platform in 2024. But while watermarks might keep Ogilvy clear of future AI rules, they might not aid consumer comprehension.

“The whole point of talent is to scale authentic relationships and credibility,” said Greene. Yet consumers, when asked, are typically cool on the idea of AI-generated glitterati hawking brands. Nearly half (49%) of Britons have a negative view of AI-generated actors appearing in ads, according to YouGov, while 92% said AI generated content makes it harder to trust information online in general.

Reed argued the system is principally an extension of Ogilvy’s “palette”, rather than a means of flooding the web with celebrity slop. “The intention is that still there is a human craft and a human behind it,” she said, adding that the agency still expects to recommend a traditional shoot when a situation demands it. “Authenticity is one of those things that is going to be even more important,” she said.

Reed said that the partnership could mean brands find it easier to work with several lower-profile names (and skip the usual scheduling difficulty) rather than shelling out for a single movie star. “I’m hoping that you’ll see more [brands] opening up to B-tier athletes,” she said.

That use case may prove particularly important to Ogilvy. CMOs place an increasingly high importance on their agencies’ ability to manage their large rosters of creators and influencers, and to do so in a cost-effective manner. Eliminating the need for a TikTok personality or podcaster to actually be on location could add a sharp edge to the agency’s current pitch.

Advertisers now demand creator metrics like cost per lead and conversions, not follower counts.

The AI platform is testing an analytics dashboard that counts how often a business shows up in ChatGPT’s answers.

Despite leaning on creators for most of the year, marketers aren’t doing it for the holiday crunch. That job still belongs to celebrities.

#7290. Senior Infrastructure Engineer II at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. For coaches, YouTubers, authors, podcasters, and other creatives, there isn't a better marketing hub to rely on to grow audiences, automate email marketing, and sell digital products — all within one platform.More importantly, there isn't a team more committed to helping creators earn a living. We're on a mission to help creators earn $1 billion using our creator marketing platform. We have always been 100% independent and 100% remote.We are proud to have built a product that our customers love, and we look for people who have enthusiasm and belief in our mission, vision, and values to join our team. We're also embracing AI thoughtfully — both in how we build and how we hire to ensure our team is adaptable, innovative, and ready for what's next.

We're looking for a Senior Infrastructure Engineer II to take ownership of a large share of Kit's core infrastructure: databases, Kubernetes, version upgrades, platform tooling, and day-to-day support. You'll also build the paved paths that let engineers, non-engineers, and AI agents test and ship changes safely.

The infrastructure supports creators who depend on Kit to reach their audiences and earn a living. When it's healthy, nobody notices. That's the job.

This is a full-time Individual Contributor role for an experienced infrastructure engineer who likes owning production systems end to end, takes on-call seriously, leans into automation and enjoys making other people's work easier.

You'll report to Mojtaba Hosseini, VP of Engineering at Kit. Day to day, you'll work most closely with Kris Hamoud and Ivan Mora Perez.

You'll also partner with product engineers across Kit, and with teams outside engineering who rely on infrastructure to get their work done. Once you're ramped up, you'll join a three-person on-call rotation, which works out to roughly one week in three.

First Week: Get access to our AWS accounts, Kubernetes clusters, monitoring, and CI/CD pipelines. Read through our runbooks and architecture docs. Take part in Get To Know Yous (GTKYs) with Kris, Ivan, and the engineering teams you'll support.

First Month: Pick up day-to-day infrastructure support requests and start working through them with Kris and Ivan as backup. Ship your first improvement, such as a version upgrade or a fix to a gap in monitoring. Shadow on-call shifts. Write down what you learn along the way, especially the parts that only lived in someone's head or those that can be automated or improved.

First Six Months: Own core infrastructure areas end to end: databases, Kubernetes, version upgrades, and platform tooling. Take your full share of on-call, which you'll start by the end of your first quarter. Become the go-to person for day-to-day infrastructure questions. Find one recurring source of pages and make it go away. Ship a first paved path, such as a sandbox environment where AI agents or non-engineers can safely build and test changes.

First Year: You're the primary owner of a large part of Kit's core infrastructure, and you lead projects in it without needing direction. Uptime stays at 99.9% or better, page volume is down, and you've found real cost savings. Your paved paths are in regular use by engineers, non-engineers, and AI agents. You've built observability good enough for AI agents to handle some on-call load. You've documented your decisions so others can build on your work.

Running production infrastructure on AWS at scale, including managed relational databases, networking, and load balancing

Operating Kubernetes in production: upgrades, capacity planning, and troubleshooting

Managing infrastructure as code and maintaining CI/CD pipelines that engineers trust

Building monitoring and alerting that catches real problems early and doesn't page people for noise

Diagnosing complex, multi-service incidents calmly, then fixing the underlying cause

Cutting cloud costs through right-sizing and reserved capacity without hurting reliability

Writing clear runbooks, docs, and decision write-ups

Nice to have: experience with Cloudflare, ephemeral or sandbox environments, or email sending infrastructure

Around 7 or more years in infrastructure, platform, DevOps, or SRE roles

Owning production infrastructure for a product where downtime costs customers money

Carrying a pager for systems you owned, and making that on-call rotation quieter over time

Leading a major upgrade or migration, such as a database or Kubernetes version upgrade, with little or no customer impact

Building self-service tooling or paved paths that other engineers actually adopted

Working on a small infrastructure team where you couldn't hand problems off to a specialist

You own what you run. You spot infrastructure debt and fix it without waiting to be asked, and you follow through until it's done.

You build quality into the work. Upgrades have rollback plans, maintenance windows are planned around customers, and "good enough" is a decision, not a default.

You leverage AI effectively. AI helps you increase quality and velocity where it matters most. You know when to delegate and when to dive in, while holding accountability.

You make work easier for everyone else. You'd rather build a tool or write a guide once than answer the same question ten times.

You teach what you know. You write things down so the next person, or the next AI agent, can build on your work.

You work in public. You share context and tradeoffs openly, including during incidents and when things go wrong.

You use AI thoughtfully. It's part of how you work, and you help others adopt it without creating new risks.

You remember who's on the other end. An outage means a creator's launch email doesn't go out, and that shapes how you prioritize.

Wanting a well-defined backlog handed to you each week. Here, you'll find much of the work yourself and set priorities with Kris.

Preferring not to be on call. On-call is a core part of this role, shared across a team of three.

You are skeptical of AI’s capabilities, or conversely use AI without good judgement and accountability.

Wanting mostly greenfield architecture. A lot of this work is running, upgrading, and improving systems that already exist.

Needing layers of approval before acting.

We're 100% remote with team members across the globe. We work mostly on East Coast time—so if you're based on the West Coast of the United States, Europe, or Asia, you might have a few early mornings or late evenings. We do our best to keep things reasonable, but a little time zone juggling comes with the territory.

This role is part of a three-person on-call rotation, so during your on-call week you'll need to be able to respond to pages outside your usual working hours.

We believe PTO should always take priority over on-call. We schedule on-call around people's lives, not the other way around. On-call is a shared responsibility, but taking time off should never be a challenge

Kit has standardized salaries based on position, no matter where you live. For this role, we're hiring at our level 4 ($197,000). Level is determined based on experience and our interview process.

Individual learning + development budget ($3,500/year)

Creator meetup budget ($50/month) to connect with creators over coffee, lunch, or other in-person meetups

Childcare benefit up to $3,000 annually through JOON

Up to six weeks of paid bereavement leave, medical leave, and disaster after six months of employment, two weeks of each paid leave in your first six months

Winter Break Closure: Kit closes for a week at the end of December, giving everyone a collective break to enjoy the holiday season. Essential support services remain available, with teams coordinating to ensure coverage during this period

Four-week, paid sabbatical after five years with the team

Fantastic in-person or virtual retreats with the team twice a year

#7276. Pernod Ricard USA Hires VML as Commerce Agency of Record

One of the largest spirits makers, Pernod Ricard USA, has named its next commerce agency of record.

WPP’s VML will handle U.S. shopper and in-store marketing for the Jameson, Absolut, Kahlúa, and Malibu brands in addition to the company’s ready-to-drink offerings.

The companies’ first work will begin with Jameson’s partnership as the official spirits sponsor of the NFL. Other work includes Kahlua & Absolut’s holiday espresso martini and Jameson’s St. Patrick’s Day program in 2028.

VML said it plans to work on creative development, point-of-sale materials, retailer-specific adaptations, digital commerce assets, and sales enablement tools. The appointment followed a review that began in March, according to VML.

Mitchell Parton is ADWEEK's senior commerce reporter, covering retail media as well as the rise of agentic and social commerce. Before joining ADWEEK in 2026, he was a reporter at Digiday Media's Modern Retail and before that wrote for several regional publications in Texas.

Camunda is the enterprise platform for agentic orchestration, enabling organizations to coordinate AI agents, people, and systems across complex, end-to-end business processes. With built-in governance, auditability, and human oversight, Camunda gives enterprises the control they need to move AI from pilots to production — safely and at scale. Trusted by over 700 organizations worldwide, including 9 of top 10 US banks, Camunda helps enterprises boost operational efficiency, accelerate time-to-value, and deliver better customer experiences.Fully remote and global, we are in the middle of something bigger: transforming into an AI-first organisation, built on our own platform. We use Agentic AI to automate, orchestrate intelligent processes, and elevate human contribution across every team.Named GP Bullhound’s Top 100 Next Unicorn list, 2025 Great Place to Work certified. Visionary in 2025 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Business Orchestration and Automation Technologies. ranked 3rd in Flexa's 2026 Most Flexible Companies, We’re growing fast and looking for top talent to join our team. If you want meaningful work, visible impact and put something genuinely rare on your CV, keep reading.

As a Senior Software Engineer, Backend on Camunda’s Distributed Systems team, you’ll help build and evolve the durable, highly available, horizontally scalable streaming platform that underpins Camunda 8’s Orchestration Cluster and powers mission‑critical processes for customers around the world.

As part of the Distributed Systems team, you will work on technically challenging and rewarding projects, such as expanding on our multi-Raft implementation, our SWIM-based cluster membership algorithm, or high performance network and disk I/O pipelines.

At Camunda, we approach engineering in a holistic manner. As such, you will form strong partnerships with product management, design, QA, and sales, and work with your peers to not only deliver but directly influence the evolution of our platform to meet our customer’s needs.

Curious about the kind of challenges you'll work on at Camunda? Watch this quick 30-minute talk from our engineers to learn more about the new Camunda Exporter and how we’re solving complex problems at scale

Design, build, and maintain backend services for the distributed streaming platform that powers the foundation for Camunda 8’s orchestration capabilities, ensuring durability, high availability, and horizontal scalability.

Lead medium‑to‑large engineering initiatives (3+ months) end‑to‑end: partner with Product to translate business problems into technical requirements, write solution designs, draft and maintain project plans, and drive timely, high‑quality delivery.

Help mature our disaster recovery and resilience story, including implementing and evolving an online backup/restore API and contributing to improved multi‑region and multi‑tenant capabilities for our cloud offering.

Design and implement developer and operator tooling that makes it easier to understand, troubleshoot, and safely operate a complex distributed system in production environments.

Improve engineering effectiveness by collaborating with DevOps and other Core Application teams on CI, testing, and local development workflows to reduce PR time‑to‑merge and the overhead of working on multiple features in parallel.

Participate in L3 support and on‑call rotations, partnering closely with Support and SRE to investigate and resolve complex, performance‑sensitive production issues, and feeding what you learn back into our product and platform.

7+ years of professional experience as a software engineer, with a strong focus on backend systems and/or distributed systems.

Proven experience leading medium‑to‑large engineering initiatives (roughly 3+ months), breaking down ambiguous problems, writing clear solution designs and project plans, coordinating with multiple teams, and driving predictable delivery.

Hands‑on experience building and maintaining highly concurrent, data‑intensive software, with a deep understanding of low‑level network and disk I/O (for example, throughput, latency, backpressure, batching, and failure modes).

Demonstrated ability to work autonomously and navigate ambiguity – you are comfortable taking on problems you haven’t solved before, breaking them down into concrete steps, and driving them to completion with minimal supervision.

Strong communication and collaboration skills in a remote‑first environment: you tailor explanations to your audience, translate business requirements into actionable technical work with Product, and help teams reach clear, decisive outcomes with minimal churn.

Experience building workflow automation or process orchestration software.

Experience with distributed systems algorithms or protocols such as Raft, Paxos, SWIM, and/or CRDTs.

Strong familiarity with the observability pillars (logging, metrics, tracing) and how to integrate observability into the development and incident lifecycle, ideally including experience with L3 support and on‑call.

Deep knowledge of the Java Virtual Machine, especially its memory model and performance characteristics in highly concurrent, data‑intensive workloads.

#7256. Terry Crews to Host the Inaugural Clio Creator Awards

The Clios, which have handed out advertising awards for more than 65 years, is finally rolling out the red carpet for creators.

The inaugural Clio Creator Awards will take place Nov. 18 at Los Angeles’ Dolby Theatre, with actor Terry Crews as host.

The ceremony marks the first year of Clio Creators, a new program presented by YouTube and powered by influencer marketing company Influential, honoring creative work across Instagram, TikTok, YouTube, and other platforms. The event will stream exclusively on YouTube.

The program has been in the works for about two years, and from the start, the goal was to produce a show on par with the Oscars and Grammys, according to Chris Detert, chief communications officer at Influential and executive producer of the event.

“We wanted red carpet fanfare,” Detert said.

Unlike most industry awards, Clio Creators will name the creator as the recipient. Creators can submit their own work, and brands can submit campaigns made with creators, according to the organization.

The honors range from Bronze to Grand Clio, and nearly every award will be handed out on stage by a major creator, including Jordan the Stallion, King Bach, and Trixie Mattel.

The organizers also plan to give Dhar Mann, founder of Dhar Mann Studios, the Clio Creator Impact Award, and short-form filmmaker Grace Wells, known for her series Making Epic Commercials for Random Objects, the Rising Star Award.

FOR Scoops, Sharp Analysis, and Insider Context

Crews and Detert first met at the Possible marketing conference in Miami, when Crews was hosting Crews Control, a YouTube series for Cadillac’s F1 team. When Detert suggested the idea, it resonated with Crews, who has maintained a multidecade career in part due to his eagerness to embrace new formats.

For Crews, who has about 25 million followers on TikTok and nearly 18 million on Instagram, the appeal is that creators are now where much of the best storytelling happens.

“Brands and ads have taken the place of sitcoms,” he told ADWEEK. “I get most of my great laughs from ads, rather than television shows. Most of the time I get it from these great creators who are doing these hilarious videos.”

Crews has already worked with Mann, one of this year’s honorees, in a philanthropic venture, he said.

The legacy of the Clios reinforces the concept that the quality of the work, rather than follower counts, is what matters most, according to Crews.

“This is not about numbers and volume,” he said. “This is about how creative these guys are. I think that is the most important thing: to treat everyone there as an artist.”

Mark Stenberg is ADWEEK's senior media reporter.

In real life, Schwimmer is also a loyal Quince customer, which made him a natural fit to appear in the online retailer’s first celebrity-fronted TV ad and the largest campaign in the brand’s seven-year history.

Launching today (Nov. 9), the campaign will run across Quince’s owned channels, social, streaming, and connected TV.

#7223. Senior ML Engineer - Malware Detection (remote work) at CloudLinux

CloudLinux is a global remote-first company. We are driven by our principles: do the right thing, employees first, we are remote first, and we deliver high-volume, low-cost Linux infrastructure and security products that help companies to increase the efficiency of their operations. Every person on our team supports each other and does what we can to ensure we all are successful.

Check out our website for more information: https://cloudlinux.com/

Imunify360 is CloudLinux's security suite for Linux web servers: real-time and background malware scanning, automated cleanup, a WAF, runtime PHP protection (Proactive Defense), a database malware scanner, and IP reputation. It runs on hosting providers' shared, VPS, and dedicated servers, so new web-malware campaigns land on our fleet early.

We sit on one of the largest labeled corpora of live web-server malware in the industry: files, verdicts, cleanup outcomes, and analyst decisions collected from production hosting servers every day. It already drives our signatures, heuristics, and ML models, and there is far more to get out of it. We are looking for a Senior ML Engineer who will turn that data into detection that ships — classifiers, LLM-assisted triage and generated signatures — and keep exploring new ML/LLM methods against it, measured by the product's false-negative and false-positive rates, not by notebook accuracy.

Our dataset focuses on web malware — PHP, JavaScript, HTML phishing kits, infected WordPress and Joomla plugins and themes, database injections — with the context around each file.

The goal is 10x more malware detected at the same or lower false-positive rate. Hand-written rules and faster pipelines will not get there; models trained on our data might.

You get an experimentation track, GPUs, and a direct line to leadership; in return, you bring numbers, not opinions.

You own the path from raw samples to a model in production, and the numbers that prove it helped.

#7220. Senior ML Engineer - Email Security (remote work) at CloudLinux

CloudLinux is a global remote-first company. We are driven by our principles: do the right thing, employees first, we are remote first, and we deliver high-volume, low-cost Linux infrastructure and security products that help companies to increase the efficiency of their operations. Every person on our team supports each other and does what we can to ensure we all are successful.

Check out our website for more information: https://cloudlinux.com/

CloudLinux Inc. develops the Imunify family of security products, designed to protect hosting environments from a wide range of cyber threats. One of these solutions is Imunify Email, an email security product that protects hosting servers against spam, phishing, and other email-based threats.

Imunify Email filters mail on about 5,000 hosting servers. Today the filter is rspamd with rules and a scoring model we train in-house. We are replacing it with a transformer we trained ourselves that runs on the customer's own CPU in under 100 MB and already beats rspamd at every operating point we measured. We are looking for a Senior Machine Learning Engineer to join our team and work alongside the engineer currently responsible for both the existing spam-filtering pipeline and the new ML model. Together, you will drive the development, improvement, and production rollout of our email security technology.

#7201. Your Brand Is Your Most Powerful Differentiator

Being an independent agency isn’t the advantage it used to be. There are simply too many choices.

And we can’t differentiate on capabilities anymore either. Most of us offer some combination of strategy, creative, media, production and now AI. And if we don’t have it all (and we’re smart), we have an explanation for how we can scale up when needed.

After more than four decades of leading agencies, I have some advice for my fellow founders and agency leaders: Don’t forget to invest in your own brand.

I know it sounds obvious. But I’ve lived through what happens when you let your own brand rest on its laurels because you’re busy building everyone else’s. The result is you end up having to work a lot harder to show people what you’re capable of, because they’ve already formed a perception.

So let’s invest in our brands so they can work for us. That starts by owning your story.

Ours began in 2004, when I led a management buyout to save our agency from disappearing. Our original 40 employees, who we still call the ‘Brave 40,’ believed in what we were building and decided to stay, and they even pooled their own money to buy the sign that would hang above our door.

We were a little agency from Rochester, New York, determined to make the industry see that great ideas can come from anywhere. We were scrappy and smart, and a little rascally. The kind of agency you never saw coming.

That spirit is still in us, but we’ve grown up a lot over the last 22 years, too. In 2012, we sold to Project Worldwide to gain the ability to scale, because their Detroit roots matched our grit, and because their independent and employee-owned model allowed us to keep our soul. As we grew, we raised the bar across our business, from the talent we attracted, to our work, and the brands we worked with.

Along the way, our brand stopped keeping up with who we’d become, and it also lost the soul that gave us our start. We needed to reflect the agency we’d become while protecting the equity and reputation we’d built.

Now, here’s something that might surprise you: I didn’t lead our rebrand.

I’ve always believed in leading from a place of abundance, and making room for more people and ideas. That means trusting the people around you to see things you might not.

I let go, and the rest of the leadership team led the rebrand with the freedom to challenge everything, including the name on the door.

Keeping my name wasn’t part of the brief. In fact, my only request was that they not call us P+N.

The team brought in outside consultants to challenge their thinking and tested the new positioning with search consultants to understand what resonated and what needed work.

One piece of feedback came through over and over: Whatever you’re saying, go further. Don’t be afraid to be provocative.

It was a good reminder that in an industry where we’re all selling creativity in one way or another, how we tell our own story is our lifeline. We can’t afford to sound like everyone else.

So while many agencies are moving away from founder names in favor of names that describe what they deliver, we went the other direction.

The team chose NAPIER because it represented a mindset and a spirit that had been with us since 2004. That founder who risked everything because she believed in her team, and the people who stuck by her to bring it to life. We’ve always had to outthink, outwork and outshine agencies many times our size. And now we’re owning our roots in a new way, with a simpler name that carries more power.

We also got much clearer about who we’re for. We’re not going after every challenger brand, we’re focused on “sleeping giants”: brands that have strong equity or scale, but aren’t realizing their full potential. These are brands who need to break free from familiar patterns and move differently. It’s what we’ve always done best, but now we have a sharper and more interesting way to articulate it.

On October 22, after 22 years as Partners + Napier, we officially unveiled our new name, visual identity and positioning. It’s been surreal to watch it all come to life, and it’s also proved what I’ve long believed about leading with abundance.

You have to know what makes you special, but you also have to give others room to see possibilities you might not. That’s how you build something that can keep growing beyond you and actually become a legacy.

We are in the business of building brands. We challenge clients to have conviction, to evolve, to get clearer about who they are and to make choices that will set them apart.

So to my fellow agency leaders: Don’t wait for a major business disruption to take a fresh look at your brand. And don’t assume that because you know what makes your agency special, everyone else does too.

Give your own brand the attention you’d give your most important client.

And give the people you’ve brought into your business the room to make it even better.

Honored by Ad Age as one of the 100 Most Influential Women in Advertising, Sharon Napier has earned her reputation as one of the most trusted voices in the creative industry. She serves as Founder of NAPIER, an integrated creative agency, twice ranked among the Top 20 Most Effective Agencies in North America by Effie Worldwide. In addition to her role at NAPIER, Sharon also serves as Senior Advisor, Global Strategic Alliances at Project Worldwide -- an independent and employee-owned holding company for ambitious agencies. Sharon is a champion of independent agencies, women in leadership, and the future of talent – sharing her perspectives on global stages including Advertising Week, the Cannes Lions Festival of Creativity, the Ad Age Small Agency Conference, the ANA Financial Management Conference, and many others. She has contributed insights to publications and podcasts including The New York Times, The Wall Street Journal, Fast Company, Ad Age, Adweek, Campaign, MediaPost, OWN IT, Great Minds, and more. In recognition of her contributions, Sharon was recently inducted into the Rochester Business Hall of Fame and has received numerous accolades including the ATHENA Award, the Adweek Female Trailblazer Award, and Working Mother of the Year from She Runs It. Sharon serves as Interim Board Chair of the 4A’s, sits on the Board of Trustees at Rochester Institute of Technology, and is a member of the Board of Directors at the Memorial Art Gallery.

Select ADWEEK as one of your favorite sources and stay up to date on our latest content.

#7187. Container Engineer at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth. More than 9 million developers and 250,000+ customers now build on Supabase, and the majority of new databases on the platform are created by AI app builders — so the systems underneath have to stay reliable while they grow fast.

Container Engineering (ConEng) owns the internal container platform that runs Supabase's multi-tenant, multi-region services — the managed Kubernetes platform that other engineering teams build and deploy on top of. We own cluster lifecycle and cluster classes, multi-cluster and multi-region topology, capacity, scheduling, isolation and runtime safety, the higher-level platform APIs, the Kubernetes deployment contract and Argo semantics, rollout primitives, and runtime configuration and secret injection.

Your job is to make the platform itself trustworthy, observable, and cheap to operate at scale — and to raise the reliability bar for everyone who depends on it. This is not a gatekeeper role. You'll make the reliable path the easy path: building the automation and platform APIs that let product teams run safely by default, instrumenting what we operate, and making sure that when something breaks we detect it and recover fast.

Tooling & ecosystem — deep knowledge of the container and orchestration landscape (Kubernetes, containerd/OCI, the CNCF ecosystem) and a clear point of view on how to compose it.

Software engineering — designing and building the platform's automation, controllers, and internal tooling as production code, including solutions that don't come pre-shaped by an existing tool.

Operational / SRE practice — the discipline (SLOs, error budgets, incident response, ORRs) that runs the platform at scale as a predictable one.

We expect you to be genuinely deep in at least one of these, and many of our strongest candidates are deep in two (usually leaning toward one). Bringing real strength across all three is rare and exceptional — and even then, no one is equally strong in all three at once, so you'd focus where you're most needed at any given time. Different profiles are welcome here; tell us which of these is your center of gravity.

Regardless of the skill set, all team members carry a share of the team's on-call rota, responding to platform incidents and driving them to resolution and follow-up.

Define SLIs/SLOs and error budgets for the container platform itself, and build error-budget policies that actually gate platform changes.

Own the Operational Readiness Review (ORR) process for new platform capabilities and major changes.

Design and track metrics for platform uptime, and increase observability into the platform by capturing the right metrics, logs, and traces across the platform layers.

Drive the incident-to-improvement pipeline: facilitate blameless postmortems, connect findings to readiness gaps, spot repeat failure patterns, and push through systemic fixes.

Build and maintain the platform's automation, controllers, operators, and internal tooling — writing production code and shipping it, not just wiring together existing tools.

Design and implement the platform-side CI/CD and rollout primitives that speed up platform deployments while working within proper change and release management.

Implement and maintain security automation for the platform: intrusion detection, automated remediation, and patch management.

Identify and quantify operational toil, and build the automation that removes it rather than just documenting it.

Contribute to the platform's continual testing automation — the test suites and pipelines that validate platform releases are fit for purpose before they reach tenants.

Act as a platform reliability and architecture expert — contribute to failure-mode analysis, dependency mapping, and resilience design for multi-tenant container workloads.

Evaluate and evolve the platform's tooling ecosystem (orchestration, runtime, networking, isolation, and observability stack) with a clear, opinionated view on trade-offs.

Improve system architecture to reduce infrastructure cost while balancing security, reliability, and performance across tenants.

Guide teams building on the platform to design sustainable on-call practices for their own services: alert quality, escalation paths, runbook coverage, and noise reduction.

Have 5+ years in platform engineering, container/Kubernetes platform operations, SRE, or production infrastructure, and have operated production systems at scale and carried on-call for them.

Are genuinely deep in at least one — ideally two — of the three strength areas below (and tell us which is your center of gravity):

Tooling & ecosystem — know the container/orchestration landscape well: Kubernetes, containerd and the OCI/CRI standards, and the surrounding CNCF ecosystem (e.g. Argo CD/Argo Rollouts for GitOps and progressive delivery; Cilium/eBPF networking and the Gateway API; Karpenter/KEDA-style autoscaling; and workload-isolation runtimes such as gVisor or Kata Containers). You reason about trade-offs rather than reaching for defaults.

Software engineering — write real production code (Go and/or a comparable systems language) and can build controllers, operators, and platform APIs — stepping back to the underlying problem and building the right solution when no off-the-shelf tool fits.

Operational / SRE practice — are fluent in SLOs, error budgets, and operational KPIs; have led incident response and blameless postmortems; and have driven down MTTD/MTTR for systems you operated.

Have worked with large-scale multi-tenant systems and understand isolation as a spectrum — from namespaces, RBAC, network policies, and resource quotas through to stronger primitives such as virtual clusters (e.g. vCluster) or VM-class runtimes.

#7180. Chief Customer Officer at Xapo Bank

Diversity is at the heart of who we are at Xapo Bank. We’re a fully distributed team of over 160 Xapiens that work remotely from 50+ countries around the world.

Our beginning: A world that enjoys economic freedom and wealth protection,no matter where you live or who is running your country.To achieve that, we search the world for the best people for the job. We work hard, think globally, and inspire each other to learn and grow. We are committed to changing the way things are done.

Although we are headquartered in Gibraltar, this is a full time, 100% remote position Work from anywhere!

Xapo's product suite has grown from a Bitcoin vault into a full member proposition: BTC and USD accounts that pay interest, a global card with Bitcoin cashback, Bitcoin-backed loans, a growing suite of BTC wealth and credit products, and access to stocks, ETFs, and stablecoins, all inside one regulated relationship.

Xapo is a bank, and a bank earns on the money its members choose to hold with it. Each of those products shipped well. What hasn't yet existed is a single owner of the economics of the customer relationship: one person accountable for winning the right members, growing what they hold with us, and keeping them, and for the balance-sheet products that make that possible.

The Chief Customer Officer is a new role at the top of the house, reporting to the CEO. You will run the stream that grows what members hold with Xapo, and you will own the Customer P&L, retail and institutional. It brings growth, client coverage, product management, and product design under one leader who is judged on customer contribution, the assets attracted and kept, and the members who stay. This is a customer-focused role in the most commercial sense: what a member earns from us, not the feature we shipped, is the centre of every decision.

The measure of success is a Customer P&L that grows on what members hold and keep: LTV:CAC improving, approved-to-paid rising, churn falling, and customer contribution the business can plan against.

This is a banking role, not a requirements-gathering role, a marketing role, or a role you can run from a dashboard. Products are priced on the balance sheet and judged on the money they attract and keep.

This part matters as much as the mandate. The known failure mode for this role, in any company, is a CCO with a polished customer-insight deck and no ownership of the number. That is disqualifying here.

Day 30: The Customer P&L baseline is documented across retail and institutional: customer contribution, LTV:CAC, approved-to-paid, churn, net paid-member growth, and assets attracted and kept. You have formed your own independent view of where members are won, lost, and under-served, and shared it with the CEO and Board.Day 60: The unified Customer operating model is live: one set of key results across Growth, Client Coverage, Mission PMs, and Product Design, read weekly from one source. Every mission PM owns a product's contribution and its price, and the roadmap is ranked on what it earns, built with Finance and the line leads.Day 90: At least one measurable improvement in LTV:CAC, approved-to-paid, churn, or net new AUM flow is live and attributable to a change you made, not a feature ship, an outcome moved. Every cell in your line has defined key results and an owner who explains the number every week.

Shape the FutureImprove lives through cutting-edge technology, work 100% remotely from anywhere in the world.

Great work-life balanceBuild amazing things with a balance of autonomy and collaborative teamwork. Set your own work schedule and make use of a flexible PTO plan when you need to recharge.

Expect ExcellenceCollaborate, learn, and grow with a high-performance team. Learn how you learn best - from books to conferences, you’ll get a yearly budget for your individual learning and development goals.

At Xapo, we prioritize consumer protection and adhere to regulatory requirements by ensuring that all Xapiens are accountable for upholding principles of fair treatment, transparency, and ethical conduct in their interactions with customers and stakeholders.

We are a tech company. A gaming company. A content company. And we do it all with passion and commitment to the game. Above all we prize our mission-driven, flat, life-celebrating, no-corporate culture, and we look forward to meeting you and learning more about what you can bring to the team.

You are passionate about chess and a meticulous linguist with a deep love for the game. Your expertise in both the English and Japanese languages goes beyond simple fluency, allowing you to appreciate the nuances and structures that create precise, high-quality translations. You thrive in a collaborative environment, are highly responsive, and have a knack for managing your time effectively to meet deadlines. With a strong background in professional translation and a high-level understanding of chess, you are the ideal candidate to ensure our content resonates perfectly with Japanese-speaking chess enthusiasts everywhere.

#7165. True Talent Solutions Partners: Senior Solutions Engineer, Digital Native and Mid-Market

Join a cybersecurity solutions engineering team supporting digital native and mid-market customers. You will bring deep engineering expertise to technical sales cycles, helping organizations integrate application security into their development workflows.

Become an expert in the application security platform and help customers integrate it into CI/CD pipelines, developer workflows, and AppSec programs.

Lead technical discovery, diagnose customer issues, and advise on solution design.

Own proof-of-value cycles from success criteria and environment configuration through technical outcomes.

Write or adapt security rules to identify vulnerabilities and demonstrate product capabilities.

Explain technical differentiation to security engineers, DevOps teams, and executives.

Improve demos, automation, and educational content to reduce customer friction.

Partner with account executives on complex sales cycles and contribute to team processes and enablement.

Travel up to 20% for customer meetings, events, conferences, and company events.

Two to five years of pre-sales engineering experience in enterprise SaaS, ideally in security or adjacent developer tooling.

Experience selling complex technical products in large organizations, such as AppSec, DevOps, CI/CD, or monitoring solutions.

A computer science degree, prior software engineering experience, and strong technical fundamentals.

Understanding of developer workflows and build systems, including CI/CD tools such as GitHub Actions, GitLab, CircleCI, Jenkins, or Buildkite.

Strong communication skills and experience engaging security engineers, DevOps teams, and technical leaders.

Must be a US citizen or Green Card holder. Applicants must be currently employed outside freelance, independent contractor, contract, or fractional roles, or not currently working.

Base salary: $147,000 to $175,000 annually. Visa sponsorship is not available.

Remote within the United States, with customer coverage on either the West Coast or East Coast.

No forms. Just smart, optimized applications.

Remember every detail and next step, without a meeting bot.
